市場焦點
美國
科技股
• 美國企業獲利維持強勁 ，背後主要受經濟展望穩健及 AI資本支出熱潮支撐 。從獲利來看 ，市
場共識預期標普500企業EPS成長率2026年達28%，2027年及2028年則分別為20%及16%，
雖呈現逐步降溫 ，但仍維持雙位數增長 。標普500預期本益比已由約 23倍降至19倍，且並非
主要來自股價修正 ，而是企業獲利預期上調所致 ，在企業獲利維持成長 、AI投資持續挹注及
估值已較高點收斂的情況下，標普500仍具中長期配置價值。
美債
外匯對沖視角
• 在全球公債遭遇拋售潮與通膨陰影下 ，美債因利率期間結構與匯率優勢 ，今年以美元計價總
回報優於多數G7主權債，儘管海外私人機構投資人正在等待長端利率落底訊號以重返市場 ，
對美元資金或以美元計價為主的投資人而言 ，當前美債名目殖利率仍處於數十年高位 ，具備

相當良好的長期鎖利價值，但價格波動風險考量下，建議債券佈局仍以中短天期為主。
台股
• 8月景氣對策燈號續亮紅燈，綜合判斷分數41分，較前月持平，對應的燈號仍是代表景氣熱絡
的紅燈，連續第9個月亮出紅燈，追平史上最長「連紅」紀錄，顯示國內經濟維持成長。
• 根據歷史統計第四季台股上漲的機率接近八成 ，除了AI投資趨勢未減 ，年底集團作帳行情以
及美國11月期中選後不確定性下降有望支撐台股多頭情緒 。群益投顧樂觀看待台股第四季表
現，有望上看53,000點。此外，彭博綜合統計台灣加權指數12個月目標價達59,688點。

就業增幅明顯放緩，降低Fed短期再升息急迫性
• 非農明顯降溫，勞動市場尚未出現全面惡化：9月非農僅增2.9萬人，遠低於預期，且7、8月合計下修6萬人；失業率升
至4.2%，但勞參率同步升至 61.8%，家戶調查就業增加 40.6萬人，顯示失業率上升部分來自勞動供給增加 ，其中營建、
製造業仍分別增加1.1萬及0.9萬人，平均工時維持34.4小時，數據呈現招聘動能明顯放慢，但尚未出現全面性就業惡化。
• 薪資增速放緩，Fed短期政策壓力下降：9月平均時薪月增僅0.1%、年增3.0%，均低於前值；同時8月PCE年增3.4%、
核心PCE年增3.0%，顯示薪資通膨壓力有所緩和 ，但整體物價仍高於Fed 2%目標，在9月Fed升息1碼後，非農報告降
低Fed在10月連續升息的必要性，市場焦點轉向年底通膨與AI投資驅動的產業動能。

• 投資建議：短期關注美國中短天期投資級債券 ，就業降溫與薪資放緩有助降低短端利率上行壓力 ，但通膨仍高於目標 ，
因此不宜過度 預期長天期利率快速回落；股市則可續關注 AI基礎建設與資本支出相關產業 ，因營建及製造業就業仍具
韌性，顯示企業投資動能尚未明顯轉弱。
資料來源：U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics， 2026.10.3

消費動能回升、通膨低於預期，內需韌性與物價壓力並存
• PCE低於預期、通膨降溫仍待確認：美國8月PCE物價指數月增0.3%、年增3.4%，核心PCE月增0.2%、年增3.0%，均低於
市場預期；其中核心PCE較修訂後7月的3.3%下降，顯示物價壓力有所緩解，但仍高於Fed 2%目標，此次年度更新回溯修
訂2021年以來資料，亦使歷史基期重新調整，後續仍須觀察通膨下降是否能延續。
• 消費回升、所得增幅偏緩：8月個人所得月增0.2%，個人消費支出（PCE）月增0.9%、實質消費成長0.6%，顯示內需仍具
韌性；惟消費增幅明顯高於所得 ，個人儲蓄率由7月4.6%降至4.1%，為2022年11月以來低點，反映家庭可支應消費的財
務緩衝持續縮小，後續消費動能仍須觀察。
• 後市觀察：PCE低於預期後，市場對10月再升息的預期明顯降溫，但長端美債殖利率仍受通膨、財政供給與期限溢價牽動

而維持高檔；建議可聚焦獲利成長與現金流穩健的優質大型成長股與短天期美債以掌握收益 ，同時留意儲蓄率下滑與 9月
消費者信心低迷可能抑制後續消費動能，動態調整部位並嚴控風險。
資料來源：U.S. Bureau of Economic Analysis， 2026.10.1
PCE與核心PCE年增率(%)走勢 八月消費支出各類別分項比較

能源與AI推升長端利率，融資成本壓力延續
• 長端殖利率上行 、期限溢價成重要推力 ：近期
美債呈現短端與長端走勢分化 ，雖市場對 10月
Fed再升息預期因 8月PCE低於預期而明顯降溫 ，
10年期及30年期的美債殖利率仍偏高位 ，反映
通膨風險 、財政融資需求與期限溢價上升 ，長
天期無風險利率維持高檔 ，並推升房貸及企業
融資成本。
• 能源與AI投資推升通膨及資金需求 ：油價維持
高檔增加通膨黏著風險 ，AI基礎建設投資則帶
動企業資本支出與融資需求 ，兩者共同提高長
期資金需求；在財政赤字與美債供給仍大的環
境下，長端殖利率可能維持較高水準 ，進而提
高股票估值折現率及高槓桿企業的融資壓力。
• 後市觀察： 短期持續觀察油價 、10年及30年期
美債殖利率與期限溢價變化；債券配置仍建議

降低長天期債券部位以降低利率風險 ，優先布
局短天期高品質債券獲取穩健息收 ，股票則聚
焦具穩健現金流 、低財務槓桿與定價能力的企
業，以降低高利率環境下的估值與融資風險。
資料來源：Bloomberg，Federal Reserve Bank of New Tork ， 2026.10.1
長天期美債殖利率曲線與期限溢價變動趨勢
資料來源：Bloomberg，2026.10.1

美光最新一季財報與財測 2026會計年度Q4實際 市場預期 2027會計年度Q1財測
營收 542.3億美元 約504.5-510.7億美元 615億美元 ±15億美元
調整後EPS 33.42美元 約31.16-31.72美元 38.15美元 ±1美元
調整後毛利率 87.0% - 約86.25%
調整後營業利益 446.4億美元 - -

營運現金流 439.73億美元 - -
AI需求 強勁 - 預期持續成長
HBM需求 產能供不應求 - 持續擴產
自由現金流 590億美元 - -
AI需求大增，美光財測超過市場預期
• AI記憶體需求強勁，美光財測顯示景氣延續：美光科技9/30盤後公布財報，2026財年的第四季營收達542.3億美元，年增

379%，調整後EPS 33.42美元，均優於市場預期；公司並預估2027會計年度第一季營收615億美元、調整後EPS 38.15美元，
分別高於市場預期的567.7億美元及36.02美元。強勁財測反映AI資料中心持續推升HBM、伺服器DRAM及高速SSD需求。
隨Amazon、Alphabet等大型科技企業持續擴大AI基礎建設投資，記憶體需求仍具成長動能。
• 長約與產品升級，支撐中長期業績：美光目前已簽署36份多年期供應協議，涵蓋約三分之一營收並延伸至2030年，有助降
低記憶體產業週期波動；同時持續布局HBM4、HBM4E及高容量DDR5等產品。公司2026會計年度自由現金流達590億美元，
並預期2027、2028會計年度供需將進一步緊俏。整體而言，AI需求已由單一HBM擴展至伺服器記憶體與儲存產品，美光財
測亦顯示景氣延續性仍強，後續市場焦點將轉向擴產速度、資本支出及AI需求成長能否維持。
資料來源：美光，彭博，鉅亨網，2026.10.1

### Page 9

兆豐銀行所做任何投資意見與市場分析結果，係依據資料製作當時情況進行判斷
惟可能因市場變化而變動，投資標的之價格與收益將隨時變動，亦不必然為未來績效表現 9
市

點
市
場
回
顧
• AI驅動獲利成長，企業基本面仍具支撐：美國企業獲利維持強勁，背後主要受經濟展望穩健及AI資本支出熱潮支撐。從獲利
來看，市場共識預期標普500企業EPS成長率2026年達28%，2027年及2028年則分別為20%及16%，雖呈現逐步降溫，但
仍維持雙位數增長。其中，AI基礎建設與大型雲端業者仍是重要驅動來源，2026年對EPS成長的貢獻合計約19%，2027、
2028年仍分別貢獻14%及11%。
• 獲利上修降低估值壓力：標普500預期本益比已由約23倍降至19倍，且並非主要來自股價修正，而是企業獲利預期上調所致。
雖然未來EPS成長率若由高檔回落，市場仍可能面臨評價修正壓力，但在企業獲利維持成長、AI投資持續挹注及估值已較高
點收斂的情況下，標普500基本面仍具支撐。因此，短期應留意獲利增速放緩與市場持倉偏高所帶來的波動，但從獲利成長

與估值兩大面向觀察，標普500仍具中長期配置價值。
AI驅動獲利成長，企業基本面仍具支撐

生產回升與價格上漲同步推動中國PMI改善
• 生產回升與價格上漲同步推動 中國PMI改善： 9月製造業PMI升至50.1%，較前月回升0.3個百分點，重返榮枯線以上，顯示
製造業景氣有所改善 ，主要受生產活動回暖及價格上漲帶動 ，中小企業景氣同步改善 ，但仍低於榮枯線，大型企業表現相對
較佳，高技術製造、裝備製造維持擴張 ，消費品PMI升至50.7%，重回景氣區間 ，另一方面，原材料及能源價格上漲推升企
業成本，上下游獲利差距持續擴大 。 非製造業PMI則由49.0%升至50.2%，服務業受節慶效應帶動回溫 ，建築業則受財政資

金投入，及基建建設推進而改善，不過需求復甦仍偏溫和，企業成本壓力尚未明顯減輕。
• 結論：第四季隨著基建投資、財政支出及房市支持政策逐步落地，經濟可望獲得支撐，但10月受長假因素影響，PMI可能短
期承壓。

台灣景氣燈號連9紅 台股Q4有望突破5萬點大關
• 8月景氣對策燈號續亮紅燈： 綜合判斷分數41分，較前月持平，對應的燈號仍是代表景氣熱絡的紅燈 ，連續第9個月亮出紅
燈，追平史上最長「連紅」紀錄，顯示國內經濟維持成長。國發會表示，AI需求令台廠積極擴廠，加上政府推動AI新十大建
設，民間投資有望維持動能。此外，受惠財富效應、企業調薪及就業市場穩定，民間消費有望持續成長。
• 法人看好台股年底前站上5萬點：根據歷史統計第四季台股上漲的機率接近八成 ，除了AI投資趨勢未減，年底集團作帳行情
以及美國11月期中選後不確定性下降有望支撐台股多頭情緒 。群益投顧樂觀看待台股第四季表現 ，有望上看53,000點。此
外，彭博綜合統計台灣加權指數12個月目標價達59,688點。
• 觀點：近期台股站上48,000點整數關卡，總市值達5.3兆美元，讓台股耀升成為全球第四大市值股市 ，顯示台股在AI浪潮下

的得天獨厚。年底前仍須留意中東衝突進展，若能源通膨壓力持續，聯準會升息預期恐為股市帶來波動，但考慮AI科技持續
發酵，建議投資人續抱台股，並以定期不定額的策略紀律加碼，目標追求中長期完整的AI趨勢回報。
資料來源：國發會，2026.09.29。

顧
外匯對沖視角—美債對沖後殖利率屬全球最低之一
• 美國國債殖利率真的高嗎？在當前全球總體經濟環境下，美國國債殖利率雖維持在數十年歷史高點附近，但若單憑名目殖利
率來判斷美債的吸引力，容易忽視匯率對沖與各國財政風險帶來的溢價差異。
• 美元視角下的殖利率比較： 若將其他已開發市場的主權債務 (如德債、日債、英債)完全對沖回美元，美債在殖利率曲線上多
數期限的實際殖利率，其實與德債並列全球最低水準。相反地，日本國債與英國國債經匯率對沖回美元後的收益率反而更高。
• 跨貨幣資產交換 (ASW)的真實回報： 以10年期主權債的對沖跨貨幣資產交換計算：美國 10年期國債有效殖利率約 4.85%，
日本10年期國債(對沖至美元)有效殖利率約5.06%，德國10年期國債(對沖至美元)有效殖利率約4.51%，這表明美國政府發

債的真實對沖融資成本其實已相對精實，且符合國際市場對各國財政基本面的合理定價。
對沖至美元後的10年期主權債殖利率
資料來源：Bloomberg，2026.9.29
對沖跨貨幣資產交換殖利率

財政基本面與信用風險—殖利率溢價反映國家債務比重
• 從歐元計價或信用風險的角度來比較 ，美債的定價邏輯非常符合總體經濟基本面： 儘管債務以歐元計價時 ，10年期美債殖
利率比同期限德國國債高出約30個基點，這反映出德國的債務佔GDP比重僅約65%，而美國則高達127%，因此美債承擔了
適度的財政溢價。儘管義大利的債務佔GDP比重高達140%且信用前景較遜色，但在統一貨幣基準下，義大利國債殖利率仍
較美國國債高出60個基點，這顯示市場並未過度壓低美債信用，美債依然享有優質避險資產的溢價優勢。
• 海外私人投資者的資金流向：近一年海外私人機構在美債加碼速度放緩，乃通膨環境下全球其他資產選擇增加，及過去升息
波段導致帳面按市值計價虧損 。然而歐洲投資人(持有一半以上的海外美債)目前仍持有龐大部位，並在等待適當的加碼時機。

• 小結與投資建議：在經過外匯對沖後，美債收益率在發達國家主權債中處於相對低位，發行成本合理且具競爭力。美債殖利
率高於德債低於義債 ，精確地反映美國政府債務負擔率 (127%)與總體經濟穩健度 。在全球公債遭遇拋售潮與通膨陰影下 ，
美債因利率期間結構與匯率優勢 ，今年以美元計價總回報優於多數 G7主權債，儘管海外私人機構投資人正在等待長端利率
落底訊號以重返市場，對美元資金或以美元計價為主的投資人而言，當前美債名目殖利率仍處於數十年高位，具備相當良好
的長期鎖利價值，但價格波動風險考量下，建議債券佈局仍以中短天期為主。
歐元計價美債殖利率高於德但低於義
資料來源：Bloomberg，2026.9.29

亞洲晶⽚債券跑輸指數，評級保持穩健
• 亞洲晶⽚債券跑輸指數，評級保持穩健：儘管AI熱潮推動收⼊創下紀錄 ，但SK海力⼠和台積電的美元債 ，今年以來的表現仍
略遜於彭博亞洲美元信用債總回報指數 ，主因為大規模資本支出擴張 ，以及潛在的投⼊資本回報率下降令 其利差承壓，美國
雲服務巨頭創紀錄的 AI支出計畫加劇了壓力 ，儘管如此 SK 海力⼠和台積電仍保持較強的高評級信用實力 ，台積電計畫投
⼊600-640億美元資本支出 ，SK海力⼠的資本支出則處於 40兆韓圜區間。兩家公司穩健的資產負債表 、合計共1,260億美元
的淨現金、強勁的經營性現金流，以及在先進製程，和HBM晶⽚領域的市場領先地位，為上述支出提供支撐。
• 投資建議：現階段台積電與SK海力⼠美元債最大的問題不是信用風險 ，而是估值與利差壓力 ，短期可能因AI資本支出過大，

及市場情緒波動而表現落後指數 ，但以兩家公司強大的現金流 、淨現金部位及產業領導地位來看 ，信用品質仍屬亞洲投資級
債中的優質標的，建議利差擴大時分批布局 。

顧 • 企業獲利成長將支撐股價上行：標普500指數的長期走勢與企業每股盈餘（EPS）高度連動。高盛預估，標普500企業EPS將
於2026年達375美元，並在2027年進一步升至415美元，對應年增率約36%與11%。在利率維持高檔、金融條件偏緊的環境
下，企業獲利仍持續處於成長軌道，將為股市提供穩健的中期上行動能。
• 本益比回落提供潛在布局契機：根據FactSet數據，標普500企業第三季整體獲利預計成長30.6%。其中，資訊科技類股獲利
成長預估高達66.5%，領先能源以外的其他產業；同時，該類股預估本益比已由今年高點回落，在獲利上修與估值修正同步
發生的背景下，浮現具吸引力的中期布局機會。
• 小結：短期市場仍可能受地緣政治與利率預期擾動，但中期趨勢仍由企業獲利擴張所主導。建議投資人採取分批布局科技股

策略，聚焦具備AI受益結構、資本支出能見度高及現金流穩健的龍頭企業。

非投資等級債券之信用評等未達投資等級或未經信用評等，且對利率變動的敏感度甚高，故本基金可能會因利率上升、市場流動性下降，或債券發行機構違約不支
付本金、利息或破產而蒙受虧損。部分基金不適合無法承擔相關風險之投資人。基金經理公司以往之經理績效不保證基金之最低投資收益；基金經理公司除盡善良
管理人之注意義務外不負責本基金之盈虧，亦不保證最低之收益，投資人申購前應詳閱基金公開說明書。
8. 基金年化配息率計算公式：每單位配息金額 ÷除息日前一日之淨值×一年配息次數×100%。「年化配息率」為估算值。當月配息率計算公式：每單位配息金額 ÷除

息日前一日之淨值×100%。
9. 基金配息率不代表基金報酬率，且過去配息率不代表未來配息率；基金淨值可能因市場因素而上下波動。投資人因不同時間進場，將有不同之投資績效，過去之績
效亦不代表未來績效之保證。本資料如以基金過去績效進行模擬投資組合之報酬率時，僅為歷史資料模擬投資組合之結果，不代表本投資組合之實際報酬率及未來
績效保證，不同時間進行模擬操作，其結果亦可能不同。
10. 部分基金配息前未先扣除應負擔之相關費用。基金的配息可能由基金的收益或本金中支付。任何涉及由本金支出的部份，可能導致原始投資金額減損。各基金由本
金支付配息之相關資料，投資人可至各投信基金公司及總代理人網站查詢。
11. 屬基金資訊觀測站ESG基金專區所列之基金，有關基金之ESG資訊，投資人應於申購前詳閱基金公開說明書或投資人須知所載之基金所有特色或目標等資訊。
警 語

## Document 030: 2026年10月宏观及大类资产月报：中美元首再次会晤，稳增长政策力度有望加码

Source PDF: all report pdf/2026年10月宏观及大类资产月报：中美元首再次会晤，稳增长政策力度有望加码.pdf
Report source: 诚通证券
Date: 2026-10-03
Pages: 22
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

宏观经济
中美元首再次会晤，稳增长政策力度有望加码
——2026 年 10 月宏观及大类资产月报 宏观深度
 大类资产表现及配置策略：
大类资产表现：9 月，上证综指下跌 2.5%，沪深 300 下跌 4.0%，创业
板指下跌 4.4%，科创 50 下跌 3.7%。债市走强，债券市场整体上涨 0.2%。
国债收益率分化，1 年、5 年期国债收益率分别上升 0.9bp、0.2bp，10 年期
国债收益率下降 1.2bp。海外港股恒生指数下跌 4.1%，美股三大股指收益分
化，纳斯达克指数、标普 500 指数分别上涨 2.6%、0.7%，道琼斯工业指数
下跌 2.6%。
A 股大势研判：全球流动性处于紧缩周期，美伊冲突、美联储货币政策
路径等外部不确定性较强，对市场仍有扰动，但国内稳增长政策力度有望加
码，对冲外部因素影响，市场或延续震荡态势。流动性层面，全球流动性处
于紧缩周期，无风险利率上行抑制资产价格。9 月美国、日本、欧洲三大经
济体央行同步加息 25BP，全球流动性处于紧缩周期。在美伊冲突未见明显

缓和、油价高企的大背景下，通胀压力或继续迫使发达经济体央行延续偏紧
的政策路径。全球范围内对美国政府债务可持续性担忧继续发酵，美国财政
部回购对美债流动性支持力度有限，油价持续处于高位推升通胀预期，美债
利率仍有上行压力。风险偏好层面，外部不确定性较强，对市场仍有扰动。
基本面方面，稳增长政策力度有望加码。央行三季度货币政策委员会例会公
报中，将下一阶段工作思路调整为“加大逆周期调节力度”，与之前相比删
去“跨周期”表述，或表明当前政策重心明确转向当期稳增长对冲。同时，
9 月中美第八轮经贸磋商达成多项共识，美国财政部长贝森特表示将贸易休
战延长至 2027 年 1 月 10 日。
A 股配置策略：外部强不确定性延续，行业层面或维持快速轮动，继续
推荐攻守兼备的“哑铃”策略。一方面，产业中长期趋势向上，AI 主线地
位不改，上游算力硬件有望维持偏高景气。另一方面，外部不确定性较强，
对 AI 高资本开支消耗企业现金流的担忧仍在，市场或继续在主线与“对立
面”之间切换，关注银行、景气度支撑或存在催化的农林牧渔、交通运输。

债券策略：9 月份经济数据或将边际改善，叠加 10 月经济季节性旺季及
国债收益率技术指标低位运行偏低，10 月国债收益率或企稳反弹。基本面看，
8 月固定资产投资累计同比跌幅虽然仍在扩大，但环比跌幅收窄，下跌动能
已经逐步衰减，并且市场已有较好的预期与消化。9 月份，BCI 指数 49.5%，
较上月提升 1.2 个百分点，指向经济有企稳迹象。此外，10 月、11 月经济表
现一般会较为活跃，也会对利率形成一定支撑。政策面看，央行短端流动性
调控维持平稳，DR001 与 DR007 均在 1.4%附近波动，流动性整体宽松。由
于我们重点跟踪的 BCI 利润前瞻指数 9 月份回升，四季度降息概率大幅回落。
技术面看，10 年期国债收益率在 1.67%附近有支撑，技术指标均在低位运行。
30 年期国债收益率破位下跌，需要重点关注短期修复情况。
 风险提示：国内存量政策落地节奏及效果需密切跟踪，增量政策出台可
能性尚存；美国经济韧性、劳动力市场稳健，核心通胀风险仍在；美联储政
策路径、表态等预测与实际情况存在偏差，欧洲、日本央行货币政策预测存

### Page 4

1、 大类资产表现回顾及配置策略
9 月，上证综指下跌 2.5%，沪深 300 下跌 4.0%，创业板指下跌 4.4%，科创 50
下跌 3.7%。债市走强，债券市场整体上涨 0.2%。国债收益率分化，1 年、5 年期国
债收益率分别上升 0.9bp、0.2bp，10 年期国债收益率下降 1.2bp。海外港股恒生指
数下跌 4.1%，美股三大股指收益分化，纳斯达克指数、标普 500 指数分别上涨 2.6%、
0.7%，道琼斯工业指数下跌 2.6%。
1.1、 市场回顾：9 月市场整体震荡调整，海外流动性趋紧，美联储加
息落地，美伊冲突扰动仍存
图 1： 9 月市场整体震荡调整；国内经济运行总体平稳；海外流动性趋紧，美联储加息落地，美伊冲突扰动仍存
资料来源：Wind，中国政府网，新华社，美国商务部，诚通证券研究所
市场回顾：9 月以来，市场整体震荡调整，日均成交金额环比继续缩量，两融
余额小幅回落。从基本面来看，8 月国内经济保持总体平稳，工业生产加速扩张，
规模以上工业增加值同比增长 5.2%，增速较上月加快 0.7 个百分点，装备制造业和

高技术制造业增势突出，计算机通信电子设备、交通运输设备、专用设备等行业增
速领先，高温天气扰动消退、外需拉动增强及存量政策落地共同形成支撑。需求端
呈现外强内弱特征，出口保持较高增速，同比增长 25%，全球制造业持续扩张叠加
AI 产业链需求旺盛，外需韧性较强；1-8 月固定资产投资累计同比下降 7.2%，当
月降幅边际收窄，基建投资发力见效，制造业、房地产投资仍处承压状态；社会消
费品零售总额同比增长 0.4%，增速较上月回落，汽车、地产链条相关消费拖累明
显，居民消费信心待提振。物价水平边际改善，CPI 同比上涨 0.8%，涨幅较上月扩
大 0.3 个百分点；PPI 同比上涨 3.8%，涨幅较上月回升 0.3 个百分点，输入性能源
价格上涨是物价回升的主要拉动因素。
9 月以来，外部环境不确定性有所上升。中美元首会晤取得部分成果，地缘紧
张态势整体延续，全球货币政策收紧节奏加快，长端利率上行压力持续，多重因素
共同作用对我国经济外部环境形成持续影响。中美举行元首会晤并达成多项共识，

### Page 5

双方贸易停战安排延期至 2027 年 1 月 10 日；美伊对峙态势未现缓和，霍尔木兹海
峡、曼德海峡扰动持续；全球主要央行集中落地加息措施，海外主要经济体国债收
益率普遍走高，全球流动性收紧压力进一步释放。
美伊冲突方面，双方对峙态势整体延续，8 月停战谈判窗口到期后未重启对话
进程，霍尔木兹海峡通行限制仍未解除。9 月上旬，双方在海峡周边仍有零星军事
摩擦，美方持续维持对伊海上封锁与经济制裁措施，伊朗则保持对海峡通行的管控
力度。冲突外溢影响持续显现，胡塞武装占领了可以控制曼德海峡的战略要地，关
键航道通行风险维持高位，对全球贸易及能源供应链形成持续扰动。9 月下旬联合
国大会期间，美伊官员在纽约进行接触，伊朗外长于 9 月 25 日正式提出七日内重
开霍尔木兹海峡的方案，要求美方履行 6 月谅解备忘录中承诺的解冻资产、解除石
油制裁、结束海上封锁等条款，美方接受后即可分阶段恢复通航并重启最终协议谈
判。但美方态度偏强硬，美国总统特朗普拒绝该提议，双方在谈判中均不愿先行让
步，核心分歧仍未弥合。截至目前，双方均未释放明确缓和信号，核问题、解除制

裁、海峡通行规则等核心分歧仍未出现突破契机，地区局势持续紧张，国际能源价
格仍处于高位。
中美经贸关系方面，中美元首会晤取得阶段性成果。当地时间 9 月 23 日至 25
日，中美两国元首于美国举行正式会晤，就双边关系、全球治理等多个领域议题深
入交换意见，达成多项共识。经贸领域核心成果方面，双方一致同意将现行贸易停
战安排延期至 2027 年 1 月 10 日，停战期间双方维持现有关税水平不变，不再新增
单边关税措施，同步推动既有贸易争端的磋商解决。双方同时认可经贸磋商机制的
积极作用，确认继续推进贸易理事会、投资理事会机制建设，落实此前达成的 300
亿美元规模对等降税安排，设立农业工作组推进农产品贸易与合作。此外，双方同
意建立中美人工智能对话机制，就技术风险、治理规则等开展定期交流，首次对话
将于年内举行。中方表示，会晤成果符合两国共同利益，愿同美方共同落实各项共
识，同时坚定维护国家主权和企业合法权益。但整体来看，本次会晤达成的多为延
续性、程序性安排，贸易休战延长时间也低于市场预期。

海外流动性方面，受主要央行加息落地、通胀预期粘性、财政压力等因素共同
驱动，海外主要经济体国债收益率普遍上行，无风险利率走高。美联储及海外央行
政策层面，9 月全球主要央行延续收紧节奏，集中落地加息措施，货币政策收紧态
势进一步确认。美联储在 9 月议息会议上宣布加息 25 个基点，将联邦基金利率区
间上调至 3.75%-4.00%。会议声明指出，通胀回落进度慢于预期，能源价格上涨与
服务业通胀粘性构成主要上行风险，美联储将维持限制性政策立场，直至通胀持续
向 2%目标回落。全球主要经济体货币政策同步收紧，进一步强化了全球流动性趋
紧的格局。
美国国债收益率延续走高态势，10 年期国债收益率突破 5.10%，盘中最高触及
5.23%，创 2007 年以来新高；30 年期国债收益率攀升至 5.45%附近，创 2004 年以
来最高水平。美国财政部于 9 月上旬宣布将单次最高回购规模上调至 60 亿美元，
但后续实际执行力度不及市场此前普遍预期，未能有效缓解改善市场流动性，长端
利率延续上行态势。市场普遍认为，短期回购操作难以化解深层结构性矛盾。欧元

区核心经济体国债收益率同步攀升，德国 10 年期国债收益率突破 3.08%，法国、
意大利等国国债收益率出现不同幅度上涨。英国 10 年期国债收益率升至 4.68%，
创年内新高。日本央行 9 月加息落地并进一步放宽收益率曲线控制区间后，10 年
期国债收益率持续上行至 3.06%，为 1996 年以来近 30 年最高水平。新兴市场经济
体国债收益率也普遍承压上行。长端利率整体走高，持续推升全球融资成本，对风
险资产估值形成压制，同时加大了跨境资本流动的波动性。

### Page 6

整体来看，中美元首会晤仅取得阶段性框架成果。美伊地缘对峙仍存，关键航
道扰动持续，能源价格波动风险尚未完全消除；全球央行加息周期延续，主要经济
体国债收益率普遍上行，全球流动性收紧压力持续上行，外部不确定性较强。
行业方面，中信一级行业指数多数收跌，30 个行业指数中，仅通信、房地产、
银行、医药板块上涨。具体来看，市场主线较 8 月有所切换，防御类板块相对占优，
周期与成长板块普遍调整，市场风险偏好有所回落。地缘层面，美伊对峙态势延续，
霍尔木兹海峡通行限制仍未解除，胡塞武装控制曼德海峡战略要地，双航道扰动支
撑国际油价维持高位震荡。但市场对能源需求预期偏弱，叠加长端利率上行的估值
压制，石油石化、有色金属等资源周期板块未现走强，随市场整体出现调整。房地
产板块受多地密集出台楼市优化政策催化，限购限贷放松、房贷政策调整等措施落
地，市场成交预期边际修复，板块迎来估值修复。银行板块受益于财政部向大型国
有银行注入核心一级资本 3000 亿元，直接增厚行业资本充足率，增强风险抵御能
力与信贷投放空间，进一步强化板块安全边际，资产质量担忧有所缓解，叠加行业

盈利稳健、高股息属性突出，在市场风险偏好回落阶段具备相对收益。医药板块在
前期估值回调基础上迎来修复，创新药出海管线持续推进、AI 制药领域催化不断，
叠加行业防御属性，获得资金关注。通信板块景气度延续，算力基础设施建设持续
推进，运营商资本开支逐步落地，光通信等细分领域需求稳定，业绩确定性较强，
走出相对独立行情。海外层面，美联储 9 月加息落地，政策立场维持偏鹰，美债长
端收益率持续攀升，全球无风险利率上行，前期表现较强的有色金属、机械等板块，
在利率上行压力下出现回调。从风格来看，金融与稳定风格领涨，周期风格领跌。

行、医药板块领涨

9 月，债市走强，债券市场整体上涨 0.2%。国债收益率分化，1 年、5 年期国
债收益率分别上升 0.9bp、0.2bp，10 年期国债收益率下降 1.2bp。海外港股恒生指
数下跌 4.1%，美股三大股指收益分化，纳斯达克指数、标普 500 指数分别上涨 2.6%、
0.7%，道琼斯工业指数下跌 2.6%。
1.2、 权益大势研判：外部不确定性较强，震荡态势有望延续
全球流动性处于紧缩周期，美伊冲突、美联储货币政策路径等外部不确定性较
强，对市场仍有扰动，但国内稳增长政策力度有望加码，对冲外部因素影响，市场
或延续震荡态势。
流动性层面，全球流动性处于紧缩周期，无风险利率上行抑制资产价格。9 月
美国、日本、欧洲三大经济体央行同步加息 25BP，全球流动性处于紧缩周期。在
美伊冲突未见明显缓和、油价高企的大背景下，通胀压力或继续迫使发达经济体央

行延续偏紧的政策路径。全球范围内对美国政府债务可持续性担忧继续发酵，美国
财政部回购对美债流动性支持力度有限，油价持续处于高位推升通胀预期，美债利
率仍有上行压力。无风险利率有望持续上行，抑制风险资产价格，新兴市场继续承
压。
风险偏好层面，外部不确定性较强，对市场仍有扰动。一方面，美伊双方继续
推动谈判，9 月联合国大会期间，美国总统特朗普表示中期选举后或达成协议，伊
朗外交部长阿拉格齐表示伊朗方面已递交谈判草案，以解除对伊朗经济制裁为前提
换取“重开”霍尔木兹海峡。但双方核心分歧仍未弥合，地缘政治冲突反复风险仍
存。另一方面，美联储 9 月议息会议延续偏鹰表态，尽管美联储主席凯文·沃什拒
绝给出前瞻性指引，但市场对于美联储 2026 年四季度至 2027 年加息预期升温，政
策路径博弈或引发市场波动加剧。
基本面方面，稳增长政策力度有望加码。央行三季度货币政策委员会例会公报
中，将下一阶段工作思路调整为“加大逆周期调节力度”，与之前相比删去“跨周
期”表述，或表明当前政策重心明确转向当期稳增长对冲。同时，9 月中美第八轮

经贸磋商达成多项共识，美国财政部长贝森特表示将贸易休战延长至 2027 年 1 月
10 日。

### Page 8

1.3、 权益配置策略：继续推荐攻守兼备的“哑铃”策略
外部强不确定性延续，行业层面或维持快速轮动，继续推荐攻守兼备的“哑铃”
策略。一方面，产业中长期趋势向上，AI 主线地位不改，上游算力硬件有望维持
偏高景气。另一方面，外部不确定性较强，对 AI 高资本开支消耗企业现金流的担
忧仍在，市场或继续在主线与“对立面”之间切换，关注银行、景气度支撑或存在
催化的农林牧渔、交通运输。
一方面，产业趋势加持，AI 主线地位不改，关注高景气涨价链条。近期半导
体硅片、晶圆代工及覆铜板产业链明确提价，受供需紧张、成本上行等因素影响，
2027 年行业涨价趋势明确且具备持续性。半导体硅片板块，12 英寸硅片主要匹配
算力、手机及 PC 先进制程需求，据台湾工商时报调研数据，2027 年 12 英寸硅片
新长约涨价确定性高，外延片价格预计上涨 15%-25%，抛光硅片涨幅更为显著，
超 40%，部分二线客户涨幅突破 50%，且 2028 年有望延续涨价态势。行业已于 8
月末迎来三年来首次全尺寸硅片涨价，6/8/12 英寸产品涨幅均超 10%。晶圆代工方

面，台积电 8 英寸厂产能利用率突破 100%，45 纳米以下制程满载，订单能见度已
延伸至 2030 年，供应链消息确认其将于 2027 年 1 月上调各制程代工价格，整体涨
幅 3%-6%，2/3 纳米先进制程涨幅更高，成熟及特殊制程实行差异化议价。PCB 上
游材料端，近期江西宏瑞兴发布调价通知，受电子玻璃布、铜箔原材料紧缺涨价及
自身产能超负荷、交期延长影响，公司全品类覆铜板上调 10%、配套 PP 半固化片
上调 15%，覆盖全 TG 系列产品，同时提示客户提前询价、合理规划备货订单。

市场或继续在主线与“对立面”之间切换。具体关注两个方向：①当前全球流动性

最强，能够较好地对冲 AI 板块风险。同时，9 月初财政部表示将于近期发行特别

央金融企业资本，推动其更好地发挥服务实体经济作用。国务院新闻办公室于 9 月

②厄尔尼诺持续发酵，农林牧渔与交通运输存在景气度支撑或催化。9 月美国
气象预报机构 CPC 表示，厄尔尼诺现象正在增强，2026-2027 年北半球秋冬期间出
现极强厄尔尼诺事件的可能性超过 90%，对农产品供给和全球航运的影响逐步显现。
一方面，厄尔尼诺通常改变全球大气环流与季风格局，美洲部分地区洪涝风险上升、
东南亚及澳大利亚干旱概率抬升，农产品价格对天气变化更加敏感。前 5 个交易日
ICE11 号白糖、上期所橡胶主力合约周度分别上涨 1.2%、4.4%。2026 年 1-7 月全
球天然橡胶累计产量 743 万吨，同比下滑 2.3%；国内合成橡胶产量 543 万吨，同

比增长 6.5%（去年同期 512 万吨，增速 11%），天然橡胶社会库存维持周度环比去
库态势。据巴西农业部数据，8 月下半月巴西糖产量 372 万吨，同比下降 4.8%，糖
厂将更多甘蔗转向乙醇生产；同期乙醇产量 25.5 亿升，同比提升 3.5%，增量主要
来自含水乙醇。若国际粮价与经济作物价格因天气预期而阶段性走强，种植收益改
善带动农户扩种、补种与单位面积投入意愿回升，进而拉动化肥、农药等农资备货
与使用，从而继续带动农林牧渔板块。另一方面，厄尔尼诺可能影响巴拿马运河、
莱茵河等航运航道，叠加中东地缘冲突导致霍尔木兹海峡、曼德海峡通航受阻，油
价上涨、航线绕行、港口拥堵等因素大幅抬升航运附加成本，航运价格持续升高。

1.4、 大类资产策略
债市：多重因素驱动下，国债收益率或企稳反弹
上月我们的观点认为，9 月份，政策对经济托底力度与节奏是决定利率走向的
关键因素，若 8 月份投资数据边际好转，国债收益率或企稳回升。
从实际走势看，10 年期国债收益率 9 月份小幅回落 1BP，整体在 1.68%附近窄
幅波动，振幅约在 2BP 左右。这整体与投资数据表现一致。8 月投资数与预期一致，
累计同比下滑 7.2%，跌幅较 7 月进一步扩大。同时，环比季调降幅 0.5%，较上月
收窄近 0.9 个百分点。投资整体有企稳回升的迹象，不过依旧相对偏弱。30 年期国
债收益率走势有所不同，截至 9 月 24 日，当月下行超 7BP，30 年与 10 年期国债
收益利差明显收窄。9 月 21 日，30 年期国债收益率跌破 8 月份的支撑位，其后连
续下行。这可能与市场交易央行节前对流动性的呵护有关。

那么 10 月份国债收益率将怎么走？我们认为，9 月份经济数据或将边际改善，
叠加 10 月经济季节性旺季及国债收益率技术指标低位运行偏低，10 月国债收益率
或企稳反弹。
基本面看，8 月固定资产投资累计同比跌幅虽然仍在扩大，但环比跌幅收窄，
下跌动能已经逐步衰减，并且市场已有较好的预期与消化。9 月份，BCI 指数 49.5%，
较上月提升 1.2 个百分点，指向经济有企稳迹象。此外，10 月、11 月经济表现一
般会较为活跃，也会对利率形成一定支撑。
政策面看，央行短端流动性调控维持平稳，DR001 与 DR007 均在 1.4%附近波
动，流动性整体宽松。由于我们重点跟踪的 BCI 利润前瞻指数 9 月份回升，四季度
降息概率大幅回落。
技术面看，10 年期国债收益率在 1.67%附近有支撑，技术指标均在低位运行。
30 年期国债收益率破位下跌，需要重点关注短期修复情况。

### Page 11

图 11： 9 月 24 日，中国 10 年期国债收益率录得 1.68%
资料来源：Wind，诚通证券研究所
黄金：价格或将呈震荡走势
9 月，COMEX 黄金期货价格震荡下行。月初价格在 4500 美元/盎司附近，9
月 24 日价格超 4320.5 美元/盎司。这主要受美元实际利率上行压制。继 8 月份，30
年期美国国债收益率突破近 20 年高点，9 月份 10 年期美国国债收益率也突破 5%。
相应的，美国 10 年期 TIPS 利率由 2.44%升至 2.83%，对黄金价格形成支撑。
目前，原油价格已经有所回落，美国国债利率继续大幅走高的概率偏低，不过
目前技术指标为明显见底，因此我们预计 10 月份黄金价格或将呈震荡走势，下跌
后反弹。

原油：价格高位回落，未来或宽幅震荡
9 月，布伦特原油价格突破 5 月新高，最高涨至 105.1 美元/桶。WTI 原油价格
最高涨至 102.4 美元/桶，与 3 月份最高价格基本持平。这主要受美伊冲突重启影响。
特别是，沙特东西输油管道（Petroline）遇袭停运以及胡塞武装袭扰曼德海峡与沙
特本土设施，对原油供应产生实质冲击。其后，随着伊朗与美国均释放谈判信号，
原油价格高位回落。短期看，原油价格或维持高位宽幅震荡走势。

汇率：美元指数上行，后续或高位震荡
美元指数（DXY）上行，月初由约 99.4 的水平升至 9 月 25 日的 101，整体相
对弱势，主要有以下原因：一是 9 月 16 日，FOMC 宣布加息 25BP，12:0 全票通
过。并且沃什讲话较前两次讲话相对偏鹰。二是美债长端利率接连突破重要点位。
9 月 14 日，10 年期美国国债收益率突破 5%，9 月 24 日已升至 5.22%。

美元兑人民币即期卖出价与美元指数有所背离。月初在 6.72 附近，9 月 22 日
之前未随美元指数一同走强，反而跌至 6.69 附近。9 月 22 日后，大幅反弹，9 月
25 日收至 6.72 附近。
美元指数在高利率的推动下仍有上行动力，不过短期利率继续大幅上行概率仍
偏小，10 月份美元指数或呈高位震荡走势。

### Page 16

2、 宏观经济回顾及展望
2.1、 存量政策效果显现，经济边际改善
8 月，存量政策加快落地，稳增长效果逐渐显现，经济运行总体平稳。工业生
产加速扩张，出口保持较高增速，当月基建投资降幅收窄，物价再次改善。
多因素共振下，工业生产加速扩张。8 月，工业生产同比增速 5.2%，高于前值
4.5%。其原因有三方面。一是高温多雨天气的干扰消退。二是出口同比增长 25%，
有效支撑了工业生产。三是“六张网”等存量政策加快落地，带动相关制造业生产
扩张。分行业看，装备制造业和高技术制造业生产扩张较快。计算机、通信和其他
电子设备制造业，铁路、船舶、航空航天和其他运输设备制造业，专用设备制造业，
电气机械及器材制造业，通用设备制造业，汽车制造业生产同比增速较高，分别为
17.2%、13.4%、11.4%、9.9%、9.8%、8.7%。
海外需求旺盛，出口保持高增长。一方面，全球制造业景气度仍比较高。全球
制造业 PMI 52.3%，连续 13 个月维持在 50%以上，中国对主要经济体出口保持较
高增速。中国对美国、韩国、拉美、非洲出口同比增速分别从 17.1%、46.6%、13.9%、

18.2%上升至 34.4%、49.3%、17.5%、30.9%；对东盟、欧盟、日本出口同比增速分
别为 30.2%、6.6%、8.2%。另一方面，AI 产业链需求旺盛。与 AI 产业链相关的高
新技术产品、机电产品出口同比增速较高，分别为 56.9%、32.8%。年内，全球信
用脉冲仍处在向上阶段，全球制造业或将保持较高景气度，同时 AI 基建尚未完成，
资本开支有望继续扩张，中国出口或仍保持韧性。
存量政策效果显现，当月投资同比降幅收窄。8 月，固定资产投资累计同比下
降 7.2%，降幅扩大 0.5 个百分点，但当月同比降幅从 13%收窄至 11%，主要是因
为存量政策效果显现，基建投资当月同比降幅从 11.2%收窄至 6.9%。由于内需仍待
提振，产能利用率处于低位，以及部分行业加强“反内卷”，企业经营预期有待改
善，暂缓扩张投资，制造业投资累计同比增速从-1.7%下降至-2.3%。虽然房地产市
场需求结构性改善，但整体商品房销售面积累计同比下降 12.1%，降幅扩大 0.3 个
百分点。整体库存压力较大，房企资金压力仍存，房地产投资累计同比下降 19.9%，

降幅扩大 0.7 个百分点。
消费者信心有待提振，消费修复放缓。8 月，社会消费品零售总额同比增速从
0.6%下降至 0.4%，季调环比增速-0.13%，均处于低位。消费修复放缓有两方面原
因。一方面，居民消费信心有待提振。城镇调查失业率从 5.2%上升至 5.3%，消费
者就业信心指数、收入信心指数均处于低位。另一方面，2026 年以旧换新补贴增
幅下降，特别是汽车消费补贴退坡幅度较大，加上上年以旧换新形成高基数，拖累
了以旧换新相关消费同比增速。汽车消费同比下降 18.5%。受房地产市场低迷以及
高基数影响，建筑及装潢材料消费同比下降 11.8%、家具消费同比下降 7.9%。
输入性因素带动下，物价再次改善。8 月，受能源价格上涨影响，CPI 同比增
速 0.8%，较上月上升 0.3 个百分点；环比增速 0.4%，较上月上升 0.5 个百分点。
CPI 增速回升主要是能源价格上涨导致的。美伊冲突再次加剧，布油期货价格同比
增速从 22.6%上升至 34.1%，RJ/CRB 商品价格指数同比增速从 24.7%上升至 32.8%。

受此影响，交通和通信 CPI 同比增速从 0.4%上升至 2.5%，对 CPI 的同比拉动上升
0.3 个百分点；交通工具用燃料 CPI 同比增速从 0.8%上升至 8.3%。输入性因素带
动下，PPI 环比上升 0.4%，前值下降 0.7 个百分点；同比增速从 3.5%上升至 3.8%，
在 7 月短暂下降后再次回升。

### Page 17

从高频数据看，9 月经济基本延续 8 月的形势。（1）出口保持韧性。9 月截至
26 日，集装箱港口吞吐量同比增速从上月的 0.31%上升至 9.6%。韩国 9 月前 20 日
出口同比增速高达 78.3%，全球贸易景气度仍较高。（2）内需结构性改善。9 月 1-20
日，全国乘用车市场零售 87.8 万辆，同比上年 9 月同期下降 22%，较上月同期增
长 8%，2026 年以来累计零售 1,259.3 万辆，同比下降 21%。9 月 1-26 日，30 大中
城市商品房日均成交面积同比下降 10.4%，降幅收窄 3 个百分点。沥青开工率同比
下降 33%，降幅扩大 7 个百分点。水泥发运率同比增速从-3%上升至 0.5%。（3）生
产分化。9 月截至 26 日，PTA 产业链平均开工率同比下降 7%，降幅收窄 18 个百
分点；汽车轮胎平均开工率同比下降 8%，降幅扩大 2.7 个百分点；粗钢日均产量
同比下降 3.3%，降幅扩大 0.3 个百分点。（4）政府债券融资增加。9 月截至 26 日，

政府债券净融资 1.6 万亿元，同比多融资 3915 亿元。政府债券加快发行将保障年
内经济平稳运行。

2.2、 美联储经济韧性通胀黏性，年内仍有加息可能
9 月 16 日，美联储加息 25 个基点，根本原因是美国经济周期和经济动量均处
于扩张阶段，失业率处于低位，通胀呈现较强的黏性。年内美联储可能再次加息。
美国经济韧性较强，失业率处于低位。美国经济周期（一阶，产出缺口）和经
济动量（二阶，经济短周期）均处于扩张阶段。一方面，经济增速整体高于潜在增
速，产出缺口长期为正，收窄速度较慢。另一方面，在 AI 资本开支、金融条件等
拉动下，经济动量（经济短周期）也处于疫情后的第二个上升阶段。9 月美国 Markit
制造业初值上升至 57%。9 月 25 日，GDPNow 预测三季度美国 GDP 环比折年率为
5%，相当于同比增速 2.3%。产出缺口为正且收敛较慢使得失业率持续低于自然失
业率。8 月，美国新增非农就业 16.2 万人，高于预期的 5.5 万人以及 7 月的 2.1 万

人。年初以来，月均新增非农就业 8 万人左右，高于保持失业率稳定所需的就业，
8 月失业率处于 4.1%的低位。
美国通胀风险仍较大。7 月美国 PCE、核心 PCE 通胀仍在 3.7%、3.3%的高位，
离 2%的目标较远。8 月，受能源价格上涨影响，美国 CPI 环比增速 0.4%，前值 0.1%，
符合预期；同比增速 3.4%，持平前值及预期值，停止下滑。核心 CPI 环比增速 0.3%，
高于前值及预期值 0.2%；同比增速 2.4%，低于前值 2.5%，但符合预期。整体而言，
核心 CPI 环比增速略超预期，其他符合预期。供需因素共振下，美国通胀仍存在三
大风险。一是美伊冲突仍存在不确定性，能源价格波动较大。二是半导体相关产品
通胀较高。8 月电脑软件及配件 CPI 同比增速从 21.2%上升至 25.4%，连续 6 个月
高于 10%。三是美国经济周期、经济动量均处于扩张阶段，周期性通胀存在上升风
险。如果经济持续扩张，通胀可能从 AI 产业链扩散至其他产品。
年内美联储仍有加息可能。美联储上调 2026 年经济增速预期至 2.3%、核心

PCE 通胀预期至 3.4%。鉴于美国经济韧性、失业率较低、通胀仍较高、政策利率
限制性不强，我们认为美联储可能继续加息。FOMC 与会者预期 2026 年累计加息
两次，2027 年暂停加息，2028 年再次降息。9 月 25 日，CME Fed Watch 工具显示，
市场预期美联储 2026 年还将加息两次，2027 年加息两次，其中 2026 年 10 月加息
概率为 64.2%。

3、 微观流动性
9 月新基金发行减少，权益类基金发行减少，主要股东资金净流出增加。
（1）基金：9 月新基金发行减少，权益类基金发行减少。截至 9 月 26 日，8
月共有 160 只新基金宣告成立，募集总规模约 445.3 亿元，较去年同期减少 849.8
亿元。从新基金类型来看，包括 85 只股票型基金，募集规模合计为 108.4 亿元，
较去年同期减少 297.1 亿元；30 只混合型基金，募集规模合计为 63.0 亿元，较去
年同期减少 99.3 亿元；23 只债券型基金，募集规模合计为 220.4 亿元，较去年同
期减少 438.0 亿元；其他类型基金，募集规模合计为 53.5 亿元，较去年同期减少
15.3 亿元。相对去年同期，股票型基金、混合型基金、债券型基金发行规模均下降。

## Document 131: 8月规模以上工业利润数据解读：8月规模以上工业利润总额同比明显回落

Source PDF: all report pdf/8月规模以上工业利润数据解读：8月规模以上工业利润总额同比明显回落.pdf
Report source: 国新证券股份
Date: 2026-10-02
Pages: 3
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

市场研究部 2026年9月28日
事件
9月 28日，国家统计局发布，1-8月，全国规模以上工业企业
实现利润总额 52719.8 亿元，同比增长 15.7%（按可比口径计
算）。
解读

## Document 164: Allianz_2026-09-17_Chokepoints and pressure points what is breaking and reshaping global energy markets_445c9ff0

Source PDF: all report pdf/Allianz_2026-09-17_Chokepoints and pressure points what is breaking and reshaping global energy markets_445c9ff0.pdf
Report source: Allianz Trade
Date: 2026-09-17
Pages: 11
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

1

In Summary 
• The ongoing oil-price shock shouldn’t prove structural, with flows likely to normalize in 
2027. But several pressure points are pulling oil and gas prices in different directions 
and the balance will decide where prices go next.  Brent jumped to nearly 110 USD/bbl 
after Saudi Arabia was forced to shut its East-West pipeline, pushing the net hit to Hormuz 
flows to about 4.3mbpd vs pre -war levels.  For now, pressure points (including strategic 
reserves near historic lows  and an underinvested refining base) are outweighing the two 
relief valves (shadow traffic in Hormuz and demand destruction). As we expect the Saudi 
pipeline to be repaired rather swiftly and volumes steady in Hormuz,  Brent should average 
87 USD/bbl in Q4 2026 before consolidating to 76 USD/bbl in 2027. But prices would hold 
near 100 USD/bbl if the outage extends toward six weeks and Hormuz remains in flux.  
• European gas prices are climbing as the region races to refill, though the resulting 
macro risk looks contained for now. Gas prices have rallied from the mid-40 EUR/MWh in 
July to over 80 EUR/MWh as Hormuz LNG loadings stay stuck at 15-25% of pre-war levels. 
We expect TTF averaging 65 EUR/MWh in Q4 2026 before easing to 36 EUR/MWh in 2027 
amid larger supply from both the US and the Middle  East. A sustained move above 120  
EUR/MWh for six months would risk tipping the Eurozone into recession.  
• A separate refining and export bottleneck is keeping refined product prices elevated. 
US and European crack spreads have surged to 62 EUR/bbl and 74 EUR/bbl. On top of 
Hormuz disruptions, a significant share of Russia’s refining capacity has been  hit by 
Ukrainian strikes, resulting in  a diesel export ban. Until June, China also had export 
restrictions in place amid fear s of supply shortage s. A structurally underinvested refining 
base is adding to the multi -layered crisis . As a result,  global oil demand is expected to 
contract 2.5mbpd in 2026 as demand from Asia has collapsed. 
• Diesel-reliant transport and agriculture, Asian petrochemicals and fertilizers are the 
most exposed sectors  with very few short -term substitutes available , while producers 
with domestic feedstock flexibility are comparatively insulated.  Between March and 
August, fossil -fuel importers paid an estimated extra USD330bn. High d iesel prices are 
hitting trucking, agriculture and construction, and the Gulf’s 40% share of global sulfur 
exports is impacting food production via fertilizer s. The n ear-term adjustment is mainly 
through inventory drawdowns and output cuts rather than genuine substitution.  
• Households are only starting to feel the full inflationary hit, and the pass -through will 
remain sticky into 2027.  In the US, gasoline alone drove over a third of August’s CPI 
increase, with pump prices set to reach 3.84 USD/gallon in 2026 (+24% y/y, about 355 USD 
more per household annually). In Europe, regulated tariffs are catching up with a lag and 
lower-income households remain disproportionately exposed.  
• Bonds are already pricing  in most of the  energy premium, but sustained inflation and 
oil-price spikes could lead to further rate stress. Much of the initial repricing has already 
occurred. We estimate that a further increase in Brent to 120 USD/bbl would only lift yields 
by 5-10bps (vs 17-18bps on inflation swaps and 8-11bps on expected policy-rate pricing). 
This suggests that a significant further rise in yields would likely require a renewed large 
supply shock or evidence that inflation is delaying monetary -policy normalization. 
• Looking ahead, over the longer run, the unfolding events  are fundamentally a 
Hormuz/Gulf shock  that will reshape global energy  markets. The conflict is reshaping 
global energy trade routes: Gulf states are building out bypass infrastructure (ADCOP, the 
Kirkuk-Ceyhan/Development Road corridor, Duqm) while China, India, Japan and South 
Korea diversify away from Gulf -transiting barrels; LNG has no equivalent bypass, so gas 
exposure remains structural.

Chokepoints and pressure points : what is breaking 
and reshaping global energy markets

### Page 2

Roller-coaster oil markets  
The recent attack on Saudi Arabia’s East -West pipeline (Petroline), combined with stalled talks between Iran 
and Gulf states on Hormuz shipping, is compounding risks, but we expect the shock to fade. Brent has spiked to 
nearly 110  USD/bbl, widening an already -significant Persian Gulf supply gap (see Figure 1): despite lower Gulf 
production, exports proved more resilient, but the net hit to Hormuz flows still stands at about 4.3mbpd versus pre -
war levels. It remains uncertain when the S audi pipeline will resume normal flows of 7mbpd - a mild scenario sees 
volumes ramp back to 5-6mbpd within weeks, while a worst case keeps the pipeline shut for about six week but we 
see this as a temporary shock either way. 
Figure 1: Supply gap estimate (mbpd)

OECD commercial stocks remain the main shock absorber, but that buffer is thinning fast. US Strategic Petroleum 
Reserve draws have slowed to just 0.3mbpd over the past month - reserves are now near their lowest level in 
decades, so Washington has little scope to repeat the scale of releases seen in 2022  or earlier this year even if the 
deficit widens further ; while Japan and South Korea are rebuilding their own reserves rather than drawing them 
down. China’s crude imports, meanwhile, are only just restarting after months of weakness and remain about 
4mbpd below year-ago levels; refiners are taking advantage of firm margins and spare quota to buy and reprocess 
crude for re-export rather than to meet stronger domestic demand, so this is not yet a genuine demand recovery. 
Against this backdrop, we forecast Brent averaging 88 USD/bbl in Q3 and 87 USD/bbl in Q4 2026 before easing to 
76 USD/bbl in 2027 - but a deeper or more prolonged Saudi export disruption, without an offsetting reserve release, 
would keep the market in deficit for longer and raise the probability of Brent holding near 100  USD/bbl.

A natural gas squeeze looming over Europe? 
Natural gas prices are ticking up as Europe races to refill storage.  European gas is exposed to the same Middle 
East disruption through a different channel: LNG loadings through Hormuz have remained stuck at 15 -25% of pre-
war levels for two months, even as Qatar sends more empty tankers into the Gulf. We expect Qatari and Emirati 
LNG exports to normalize only in Q1 2027, so for TTF to keep pricing enough Asian LNG demand out of the market 
to free cargoes for Europe, prices must stay elevated for longer: the rally from the mid -40 EUR/MWh in July to over 
80 EUR/MWh has already cut some demand from the rest of the world. With Europe still playing catch -up on 
storage (see Figure 2), we expect TTF to average 65 EUR/MWh in both Q3 and Q4 2026 before consolidating to 36 
EUR/MWh in 2027 as Hormuz flows normalize and incremental US LNG supply arrives. The key near -term risk is 
storage: a cold winter combined with a lower-than-usual fill could deplete storage before spring, front-loading price 
pressure into early 2027. 
Figure 2: European gas storage

### Page 4

4

For now, the macro risk from gas prices looks contained.  In 2022, gas prices averaged close to 130 EUR/MWh for 
a full year, yet the Eurozone still grew about 3.4%, cushioned by a post -pandemic demand boom and a fiscal 
response worth an estimated 0.4pp of growth. Today’s backdrop is less forgiving: fiscal space has narrowed and 
household demand is far less dynamic. We think TTF in the 80 -100 EUR/MWh range could be absorbed for nine 
months or more without a growth shock, but a sustained move above 120 EUR/MWh for six months or longer would 
tip the Eurozone into recession. Our base case, TTF averaging 65 EUR/MWh through Q3 and Q4 2026, does not test 
that threshold, though a winter upside scenario would keep the tail risk alive.  
Refined products to remain expensive  
A two -layer infrastructure bottleneck is hitting refined products and will keep driving regional prices up, 
independent of crude.  In August, Brent averaged 89  USD/bbl, yet clean -product crack spreads surged to record 
levels, the US benchmark reached 62  EUR/bbl and European diesel crack 74 EUR/bbl (68% and 92% above their 
five-year averages) showing the main story is refining availability, not crude input costs. Three regions are driving 
the crunch. In the Middle East, the US -Iran conflict and Hormuz blockade have hit both refinery operations and 
product exports: pre -conflict flows of roughly  6mbpd through the Strait have fallen to 1.1mbpd, and the Saudi 
pipeline attack compounds this by also halting flows to the Yanbu refineries. In Russia, Ukrainian strikes cut refinery 
runs to multi -year lows by mid -May, and Moscow’s diesel export ban - extended through September, with no 
guarantee against further extension - has squeezed a market that depends on Russia for 11% of global diesel. In 
China, export restrictions imposed in March to preserve domestic inventories cut clean -product exports by 24.3% 
year-on-year over the first five months, though Beijing’s June release of 13mn tons should ease global supply, lifting 
China’s exports full-year total to 32mn tons, 0.6% above last year. 
Figure 3: US and Europe crack spread historical prices (USD/bbl)

Sources: 
 LSEG Workspace
 , Allianz Research

Global clean-product inventories are drying up as the crisis extends through 2026.  European (ARA) inventories 
are well below the five -year average. In the US, refiners are running at record utilization (98% in September), but 
most of the extra output is being exported for higher margins rather than sold domestically — gasoline stocks are 
6% and diesel 14% below seasonal norms, and US diesel topped 6  USD/gallon for the first time. Rising domestic 
prices could eventually pull barrels back into the US market and curb these exports, with some early – tough strongly 
pushed back by government officials for now  - talks about potential diesel export restrictions to support domestic 
supply. Additionally, there is little relief from spare capacity: years of underinvestment and refinery closures have 
left the West structurally unprepared, global refining investment is on track to fall below 40  USD bn this year (-15% 
y/y), and capacity is set to grow only marginally through 2040. Unlike 2022, when Europe and Asia reallocated 
capital toward LNG regasification within months, refining capacity cannot be added quickly enough to ease this 
shortage in the short or medium term, and rebuilding inventories will take time even once Hormuz flows normalize. 
We now expect world oil demand to contract by 2.5mbpd in 2026 as high product prices force further demand 
destruction, concentrated in middle distillates and petrochemical feedstocks in Asia. Longer term, persistently 
expensive products should accelerate substitution toward EVs and heat pumps, and put a premium on refineries 
with the flexibility to shift crude grades and product mix.

Downstream sector impact and potential substitution 
After six months, duration, and not just the peak price, is now the central macro risk. Between March and August, 
fossil-fuel importers paid an estimated 330  USD bn more than pre-war forward prices implied, split roughly evenly 
between crude and refined products - a real income transfer from importers to exporters that squeezes household 
purchasing power and corporate margins, weakens currencies, and strains public finances wherever governments 
subsidize fuel. The Hormuz crisis is unusual in disrupting many linked -but-hard-to-substitute commodities at once 
(crude, gas, LPG, naphtha and sulfur ) spilling into fertilizer and petrochemical shortages. Substitution can happen 
at three levels : (i) switching energy carriers, (ii) switching industrial feedstocks, or (iii) consumers switching final 
products. With the first working fastest but simply redistributing scarcity, and the other two developing more slowly 
but reshaping industrial structure permanently. Inflation itself spreads in three stages: a fast first -round hit to 
energy-intensive industries (speed depending on taxes and subsidies), a slower production -cost effect as old-price 
inventories and contracts run out, and a second -round effect as wages, inflation expectations and price -setting 
behavior adjust. 
The immediate impact is on energy -intensive industries.  Gulf states are major exporters of both fuels and 
feedstocks: Europe is less directly exposed to Gulf crude than East Asia but still feels it through global oil prices, LNG 
competition and product shortages; the US is more insulated on crude but exposed via global product prices, hitting 
lower-income consumers hardest while producers and refiners gain from higher margins. East and South Asia face 
the largest physical exposure, with China, India and Japan recording the highest net additional fossil -fuel import 
costs since the conflict began – USD 31.7 bn, USD 14.4bn and USD 10.4bn respectively. Diesel is the most powerful 
transmission channel given its role in trucking, agriculture, mining and construction - sectors that are hard to 
electrify, especially for long -haul transport and in emerging markets - while shipping and aviation face higher 
bunker/jet-fuel costs and rerouting. Among industrial inputs, East Asian petrochemical crackers reliant on imported 
naphtha are most exposed, whereas the US benefits from domestic ethane and China retains partial cover via coal-
based chemicals; metals, cement and glass are exposed through electricity and high-temperature heat costs, hitting 
aluminum hardest. Gulf countries supply more than 40% of global sulfur exports and about 20% of 
ammonia/nitrogen-fertilizer exports, so the crisis strikes food production through fertilizer, diesel, irrigation and 
packaging costs simultaneously. 
The second-round effect on the broader economy will be felt with a lag and last longer.  Food shows this best: 
packaged food embeds fertilizer, farm fuel, irrigation, refrigeration, processing heat, packaging, transport and 
labor, each repricing at a different speed : fresh produce reacts within weeks, grain can take up to a year via fertilizer 
and harvest effects, and restaurant prices adjust slowest but most persistently as labor and rent costs catch up. 
Large retailers can hedge and absorb costs better than small one s, and real household income falls as consumers 
trade down and defer spending. Export restrictions, subsidies and price controls can temporarily shield domestic 
food prices but tend to redistribute scarcity and worsen the burden on lower -income, food-importing economies.

10
20

Asia North America Europe Middle East

### Page 6

6

Construction and real estate are hit both directly (cement, plastics, metals, diesel) and, more durably, indirectly 
through higher interest rates raising financing costs and lowering the present value of future rents. Compared with 
2022, the economy enters this shock with more renewable capacity and European LNG infrastructure - renewables 
installed since 2020 reportedly avoided USD 36bn of fossil -fuel imports in the first five months of the crisis - but 
public debt is higher and buffers are thinner after the pandemic and the 2022 crisis.  
Substitution will spread across fuels, feedstocks and products but initially redistributes inflation rather than 
eliminating it. The first response is inventory drawdowns, switching to costlier available inputs, output cuts and cost 
pass-through. Over time it becomes more structural, reorganizing downstream markets around flexibility, lower 
energy intensity and redundancy - persistently expensive oil and gas improve the economics of electrification, even 
though electrification changes commodity dependence rather than removing it. Because capital, regulatory 
approval and margins all constrain the pace of change, expect a two -stage adjustment: curtailment and demand 
destruction first, structural substitution and capex later , alongside rising demand for fuel/feedstock flexibility even 
where it costs more than a specialized, single-fuel plant. 
Figure 5a and 5b: Fuel potential substitution in energy-intensive sectors in the short-term; energy-intensive sector 
inputs (left) within share of total inputs in specific sectors (% - right)

Source: Allianz Research
 , Eurostat, Oxford Economics  
Households to face higher inflation 
Six months in, households are losing purchasing power in stages.  Petrol and diesel reprice within days or weeks, 
but gas and electricity bills follow regulated tariff calendars and contract -renewal dates, so the current inflation 
reading is not yet the peak. Exposure varies sharply by household - an EV- and heat-pump-owning homeowner is 
comparatively insulated, a gas-heated renter won’t feel the full effect until tariffs reset, and a rural household reliant 
on diesel cars feels it immediately. Petrol and diesel are the fastest channel: European pu mp prices typically pass 
through crude costs within one to two months, roughly one -for-one. In the US, gasoline alone drove over a third of 
August’s CPI increase, diesel peaked above 6  USD/gallon in September, and average pump prices are on track for 
3.84 USD/gallon in 2026 (+24% y/y) - about USD 355 more per year for a typical household; hitting rural, low-income 
households hardest given limited access to public transport.  
Gas and electricity bills rise on institutional timetables, so the pass -through is felt with a lag that varies by 
market. In France, the regulated gas reference price rises 6.3% on October 1 (to 182.88  EUR/MWh including tax, 
about 60 EUR/year for indexed contracts), though roughly 40% of households are on fixed-price contracts and won’t 
feel it until renewal; French electricity moves more slowly, with the next adjustment scheduled for February 2027. 
The UK’s quarterly price cap rises by a bout 5  GBP/month from October, partly offset by government VAT relief, 
while roughly 35% of households on fixed contracts are shielded for now. Japan and India rely more on subsidy 
calendars than market pass -through, which smooths the household shock across time wit hout removing its fiscal 
cost. More broadly, real energy bills were still about 4% above 2019 levels in 2024, so this shock lands on budgets 
that had not fully recovered from 2022 ; and unevenly: an estimated 120mn households in advanced economies 
spend over 10% of income on home energy, rising to 20% including transport for the poorest, and the bottom income 
quintile spent 12% of disposable income on gas, electricity and heating in 2022 versus 4% for the top quintile.

In the longer term, the energy crisis impacts inflation beyond energy bills.  A second inflationary wave builds as 
transport and industrial costs feed into food, goods and services with a lag of around two years: a permanent 10% 
rise in oil prices raises headline CPI by about 2.3%, food CPI by 0.3% and core CPI by 0.1%, and after e xpenditure 
weights, headline CPI rises by almost 0.4% - consistent with the roughly 0.5% inflation contribution the last oil move 
added in the Eurozone, UK and Canada. Looking ahead, households are likely to weigh energy resilience more 
heavily in purchasing decisions, shifting spending from variable fuel cost s toward upfront capex on electrification 
and insulation - even as higher prices simultaneously squeeze the ability to finance that switch, widening inequality 
in energy exposure. Electricity is set to become the central household energy price, trading oil and gas exposure for 
exposure to power prices, network ch arges and time -of-use pricing, with government subsidies and price -cap 
calendars still deciding when the crisis is actually felt.

The energy-yield loop 
Energy shocks often have an immediate effect on rates through several components.  The first one is inflation 
expectations: Higher commodity prices tend to increase inflation and are priced directly on the derivatives market . 
Historically a 1% rise in Brent lifts the 2Y inflation swap by 1. 65bps in the US and 1. 77bps in the Eurozone, and the 
1y1y forward inflation swap by 0.64bp in the US and 1.09bp in the Eurozone. During special shocks the effect 
intensifies: The US 2Y inflation swap moved 1. 86bps per 1% of oil during the Ukraine shock and 2. 01bps during the 
Iran episode. The EU 2Y inflation swap moved by 2.60bps during the Ukraine shock, but only 0.36bp during the Iran 
episode. 
The first channel then impacts  the expected short rates path.  When inflation tends to increase suddenly, the 
central bank faces more pressure  to be hawkish because of market pricing . Historically a 1% rise in Brent adds 
1.02bp to US monetary pricing (proxied by 2Y OIS − Fed Funds) and 0.83bp to EUR monetary pricing (proxied by 2Y 
OIS − €STR). During crises , this pressure concentrates region by region: US pricing repriced hardest during the 
Ukraine shock at 2.82bps per 1% of oil, close to three times its long -run sensitivity, while in the current Iran episode

it is EUR pricing that dominates at 2.40bps. This second channel tends to move short rates (more sensitive to inflation 
and monetary policy) (<2Y maturities: US 2Y +0.95bp, DE 2Y +0.53bp per 1% of oil) and the "belly" (more sensitive 
to growth and the economy) (5Y –7Y: US 5Y +0.98bp, DE 5Y +0.54bp) fa ster than long rates (which embed more 
fundamental factors such as demography, fiscal situation, political stability) (>10Y maturity: US 30Y +0.65bp, DE 
30Y +0.50bp). When the front end increases more than the long end, it induces a bear flattening , which hurts the 
economy more broadly. This tends to tighten financial conditions by increasing borrowing costs, slowing the growth 
of production and credit, but also by increasing transport costs, making it harder for commodities trading houses to 
provide more inputs to markets due to their short -term financing at 3M.

In times of shocks, the sensitivities of commodities on rates roughly double . On the German curve, a 1% rise in 
Brent moves the 2Y yield by 0.53bp historically versus 1.43bp in the current episode, and EUR monetary pricing by 
0.83bp versus 2.40bp. Per +10% move in Brent, that is about +5bps on the German curve and +9bps on the US curve 
in normal times, rising to about +14bps on the German 2Y and +12bps on the US 10Y in shock, with the 2Y inflation 
swaps moving some + 17bps historically and up to + 20bps ina  shock.  Figure 9 illustrates the incremental impact 
on rates and inflation markets implied by a move in Brent to USD120, USD100 or USD80 from current levels. 
These scenario moves are separate from the repricing already observed during the current episode, much of 
which is already embedded in inflation compensation and monetary-policy expectations.

### Page 9

9

Bond markets have already absorbed a large portion of the current energy shock through higher inflation 
compensation and a repricing of the expected monetary -policy path. While additional oil-price gains would still 
exert upward pressure on rates, the scenario analysis suggests that the marginal impact is now smaller than during 
the initial phase of the shock. As a result, a sustained and significant further rise in yields would likely require a new 
escalation in supply disruptions or clearer evidence that energy -driven inflation is feeding into broader price 
dynamics and delaying the normalization of monetary policy.  
How the conflict is reshaping the global energy map 
The Strait of Hormuz has always been the Gulf’s structural vulnerability, and this crisis is accelerating a shift 
from managing chokepoint risk to actively engineering around it.  The attack on Saudi Arabia’s own hedge (i.e.  
Petroline) is a reminder that existing bypass infrastructure is now a target, not a guarantee. Riyadh’s repair timeline 
will determine how quickly that redundancy returns, but the episode has already revived talk of expanding capacity 
rather than just repairing it. The UAE’s ADCOP pipeline to Fujairah, which sits outside the Strait entirely, remains the 
cleanest working bypass and has absorbed a large share of redirected flows, with further capacity additions 
planned for 2027. Iraq is leaning on the Kirkuk -Ceyhan pipeline and the broader Development Road corridor to 
Türkiye, while Oman’s Duqm port is the other candidate for a Gulf pipeline that bypasses the Strait altogether.

There is no easy fix for natural gas.  Unlike crude, LNG cannot be pipelined around Hormuz, which is why Qatari 
and Emirati LNG loadings have collapsed even as oil exports partially recovered via redirection. With normalization 
pushed out to Q1 2027, Qatar’s pattern of sending more empty tanke rs into the Gulf is one of the few signs of an 
eventual ramp-up, but there is no Petroline-equivalent for gas and the exposure is structural. 
On the demand side, the pressure to de -risk predates this crisis — it dates back to the war in Ukraine — and is 
now intensifying, though Russia/Ukraine plays no direct role in the current Hormuz shock beyond the refined -
products channel already noted.  China has spent the past decade building overland alternatives (the ESPO 
pipeline from Russia and the Kazakhstan -China link) specifically to cut seaborne Gulf and Malacca dependency, 
and its crude imports remain about 4mbpd below 2025 levels even at firm prices — though the recent uptick in 
Chinese buying is a refining -margin and re -export story more than fresh demand. India is diversifying toward US, 
West African and discounted Russian barrels via the Cape route while expanding strategic reserves, and Japan and 
South Korea are rebuilding SPRs drawn down earlier in the crisis. The India -Middle East-Europe Economic Corridor 
is likely to see fresh political momentum as a longer-term, non-maritime link between the Gulf and Europe. None of

### Page 10

10

this displaces Hormuz as the region’s central artery near -term, but it marks a durable shift toward supply -side 
redundancy and demand-side diversification that will outlast the current conflict.

After years of declining building permits, the ﬁrst half of 2026
brought a welcome improvement, with permit activity
returning to growth. Despite this rebound, permit volumes
remain historically low, and renewed geopolitical tensions in
the Middle East add new headwinds for the construction sector
Alissa Lefebre
Belgian building permit growth strengthened in early 2026, but higher energy
prices and rising ﬁnancing costs could curb momentum
Recovery in building permits continues in

ﬁrst half of 2026
The ﬁrst half of 2026 brought a welcome improvement in building
activity, with the number of building permits rising by 8%
compared with the same period a year earlier. The recovery was
mainly driven by new construction permits, which increased by
12% year-on-year, while renovation permits also posted a more
modest gain of 5%. However, the picture is mixed across market
segments. In the renovation market, growth was conﬁned to
residential projects, where permits increased by 6% compared to

Higher energy prices risk derailing this
recovery
Higher energy prices feed through to the construction sector, as
building materials are highly energy-intensive to produce.
According to the Belgian government's construction material price
index, building material prices were already up 8% in July 2026
compared with a year earlier. Some categories recorded even
stronger increases: prices for precious and nonferrous metals, for

instance, rose by as much as 36%.

## Document 237: GLOBAL MARKET CALL_ Bond Vigilantes Gone Wild

Source PDF: all report pdf/GLOBAL MARKET CALL_ Bond Vigilantes Gone Wild.pdf
Report source: Substack Reports
Date: 2026-10-04
Pages: 9
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

From:    Yardeni QuickTakes <yardeni-research@ghost.io>
Subject: GLOBAL MARKET CALL: Bond Vigilantes Gone Wild
Date:    10 hours ago
GLOBAL MARKET CALL: Bond Vigilantes Gone Wild

YARDENI QUICKTAKES
GLOBAL MARKET CALL: Bond
Vigilantes Gone Wild
By Ed Yardeni & Toby Hearst • 4 Oct 2026
View in browser

### Page 2

Photo by Jené Stephaniuk / Unsplash
The Bond Vigilantes have gone wild worldwide, pushing government bond
yields higher in developed and emerging markets alike.
A month ago, we asked whether rising yields reflected stronger growth,
higher inflation, or looming fiscal crises. We still think the answer is mostly
growth. The exception is where government finances are weakest. There,
bond investors are charging a fiscal-risk premium. France may be on the

verge of a full-blown debt crisis.
Here's more:

### Page 3

I. Global Bond Yields
US Treasury yields rose on Friday even though September payrolls
increased only 29,000. The 10-year US Treasury yield ended the week at
5.28%, after hitting its highest level since 2002 midweek (chart). Higher
real yields account for nearly all of this year's increase, while inflation
expectations have barely budged. In the UK and Australia, 10-year yields
are higher still, at 5.37% and 5.36%. Japan's is 3.09% and still climbing.

The French government now pays more than Italy or Greece to borrow for
10 years (chart). The yield spread between the French and the German
10-year government bonds jumped on Thursday, when France unveiled a
2027 budget that freezes public-sector pay and most pensions to limit the
budget deficit to 5% of GDP. The spread is now the widest since the
Eurozone debt crisis of 2012. German yields fell last week as French
yields rose. Inflation is running hot in both countries, so the gap reflects

France's finances.

### Page 4

Six of the 22 bond markets on our list have seen 10-year yields climb
100bps or more this year (chart). France leads at 131bps, with the US
second at 112bps. Italy, Indonesia, Japan, and South Korea round out the
group.
Two-year government note yields are above central banks' official policy
rates in all six economies on our chart, by as much as 107bps in the UK
(chart). The gap is smallest in Australia, at 34bps. The Reserve Bank of
Australia raised its cash rate by 25bps to 4.60% on September 29, its

fourth hike this year.

### Page 5

II. Global Stocks
South Korea's ETF has nearly doubled this year, up 97.4% in dollars, with
Taiwan's up 83.1% (chart). The Emerging Markets ex-China ETF (EMXC)
is up 39.4%, and its lead over the broader Emerging Markets ETF (EEM)
widened to 15.7 percentage points last week from 14.9. We continue to
prefer EMXC over EEM. France's ETF is down 6.1%, the weakest
developed market on our list. The US ETF (SPY) is up 12.9%, in the top
half of the rankings.

### Page 6

Stay Home vs Go Global match has been in a stalemate in recent months.
The US ETF trails the All Country World ex-US ETF (ACWX) by only 0.3
points ytd, versus 1.0 a week earlier. The MSCI USA ETF (PBUS) and
ACWX have moved in lockstep, and both pulled back from record highs
last week (chart).
III. Currencies
The dollar has rebounded off the bottom of its long-term uptrend channel.
The US Dollar Index (DXY) is at 101.9, up from 99.2 at the start of

September (chart). We have expected the dollar to stay firm, and it has.
The euro's slide accounts for much of the dollar's gain, since the euro
carries a 57.6% weight in the DXY. The euro is trading at $1.13, its lowest

### Page 7

since May 2025 (chart). It fell sharply on Thursday, alongside French
bonds.
The yen is trading at 157.68 per dollar, near the middle of its 153-160
range since the joint US-Japan intervention in late July (chart). Japan's
latest data show no intervention since late August.
IV. Weekly Focus
(1) Seaborne crude oil shipments exiting the Gulf have rebounded to
roughly 16.5-17.5 million barrels per day (averaging around 98% of pre-
war levels, excluding Iran). Major producers like Saudi Arabia have driven

much of this rebound. While crude flows have recovered, refined fuels like
diesel face severe ongoing bottlenecks. Refined product flows remain a
fraction of pre-war norms (hovering well below historical averages),

### Page 8

keeping global diesel supplies and pricing tight. On Friday, the G7
announced the coordinated release of up to 100 million barrels of
emergency oil and diesel inventories.
(2) Inflation data across Europe underscored lingering stickiness, with
annual prints registering at 3.3% in Germany, 3.0% in France, 4.1% in
Italy, and 4.9% in Spain.
(3) In Asia, a hotter-than-expected Tokyo-area inflation report reinforced
expectations of further policy normalization by the Bank of Japan (BOJ).

Tokyo’s core CPI (excluding fresh food) jumped to 2.7% y/y in September,
accelerating sharply from 1.8% in August and pushing inflation back
above the BOJ’s 2.0% target for the first time in several months (chart).
The core-core measure climbed even higher, to 3.0% (up from 2.0% in
August). Services inflation also rose notably, to 2.3%.

### Page 2

2
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
The monthly household spending indicator was unchanged
in August, with the annual pace of growth easing to 6.8%yr.
Today’s result was somewhat stronger than Westpac’s
expectation for a 0.5%mth decline, but weaker than the
market expectation of a 0.4%mth increase. It also marks a
material slowing in the pace of spending growth following a
run of stronger outcomes since May.
The strength recorded in services spending over recent months

gave way in August, with spending falling 0.3%mth (+5.9%yr).
This was offset by a modest 0.3%mth rise in goods spending,
taking annual growth to 7.7%yr, its strongest pace since
November 2022. As highlighted in our latest Westpac–DataX
Card Tracker (see here), there has been a modest rotation
away from discretionary categories. Discretionary spending fell
0.3%mth (+7.2%yr), while non-discretionary spending offset
some of this weakness, rising 0.6%mth (+6.3%yr).

At the detailed level, recreation & culture recorded the
largest decline, falling 1.4%mth, with the ABS noting that
some of the weakness reflected payback following elevated
spending associated with major sporting events in previous
months. Clothing & footwear (–1%mth), alcoholic beverages
& tobacco (–1%mth), health (–0.9%mth), furnishings &
household equipment (–0.6%mth), and food (–0.3%mth) also
declined. Offsetting some of this weakness were gains across

miscellaneous goods & services (+0.3%mth), and hotels, cafes
& restaurants (+0.8%mth). The major offset, however, came
from transport spending, up 2.3%mth. The ABS attributed the
increase to stronger fuel spending and new vehicle purchases,
particularly electric vehicles. Higher fuel prices, together with
the full restoration of fuel excise from 3 August, helped drive
an 8.1% increase in fuel spending. Excluding fuel spending,
total household spending would have fallen 0.3%mth.

State outcomes were mixed. Tasmania (–0.5%mth), Victoria
(–0.4%mth) and WA (–0.2%mth) recorded declines, while SA
(+0.1%mth), NSW (+0.3%mth) and Queensland (+0.3%mth)
proved more resilient.
Today’s result points to a moderation in the recent strength
in household spending. Our Westpac Card Tracker suggests
September has been about flat as well, albeit with nominal
spending still posting robust growth for the quarter as a
whole. Some of the quarterly gain appears to reflect higher

prices rather than stronger underlying activity. Meanwhile, part
of the slower monthly pace – and shift from discretionary to
non discretionary – appears to be due to renewed constraints
from higher fuel prices, concerns around further interest
rate increases and ongoing weakness in housing sentiment,
themes that are also evident in consumer sentiment, which
deteriorated further through September (see here).

## Document 263: Next Generation EU_ The programme is coming to an end, but its effects on economic activity will endure (2026-09-23)

Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-09-23
Pages: 3
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

23 September 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
NEXT GENERATION EU: THE PROGRAMME IS COMING TO AN END, BUT ITS EFFECTS ON ECONOMIC ACTIVITY WILL
ENDURE
Guillaume Derrien
A pillar of the NextGenerationEU programme, the Recovery and Resilience Facility (RRF) will expire on December 31, 2026. As of the
end of August 2026, slightly more than three-quarters of the total budget had been disbursed (EUR 440 billion out of EUR 573 billion).
Disbursing the remaining quarter (EUR 133 billion) by the end of the year will require a significant increase compared with the histo-

rical pace (EUR 90 billion per year). The goal of channeling these funds toward productive investment appears to have been achieved.
Tangible results for investment in Europe
According to Eurostat, public spending co-financed by the RRF totaled EUR 310 billion between 2020 and 2025. Nearly three-quarters (74%)
consisted of capital expenditures, including EUR 106 billion in direct public investment and EUR 122 billion in capital transfers to the private

sector (such as grants). This spending supported investment by businesses and households.
The list of major programmes funded by the RFF, published by the European Commission 1, shows that, within this scope, nearly two-thirds of
the funding went to transportation (primarily rail), energy infrastructure, and digital infrastructure (see Chart 1).
Another notable point: capital expenditures co-financed by the RRF increased significantly, reaching 0.4% of GDP for the European Union as a

whole in 2025 (see Chart 2). There are significant disparities among Member States, primarily due to differences in the amounts allocated to
them. Croatia (1.9%), Greece (1.2%), Italy (1%), and Spain (0.7%) received significant support, in contrast to France and Germany (0.1%). These
figures are consistent with the IMF’s estimate that the RRF added approximately 0.3 percentage points (pp) to EU growth in 2025 (more than
1 pp in Greece and Croatia, around 0.8 pp in Italy, and 0.5 pp in Spain) 2. Furthermore, as the first major joint European borrowing initiative, the

RRF made it possible to finance investment without increasing national budget deficits, giving several countries (Spain, Italy, Greece, Portugal)
valuable breathing room to consolidate their public finances.
1 Recovery and Resilience Scoreboard
2 The EU’s Recovery and Resilience Facility: Experience and Lessons for the Next Multiannual Financial Framework, WP/26/114, June 2026
SOURCE: EUROPEAN COMMISSION, Recovery and Resilience Scorecard

What impact on EU’s economic activity can we expect once this programme ends?
At first glance, effects will be limited. The end of the programme is expected to weigh on both public and private investment. However, the
strengthening of economic activity that we anticipate (see details in our latest Ecoperspectives – Q3 2026) rests primarily on German public

investment programmes and structural drivers (investment in AI, defence spending).
The ECB makes the same point in its September macroeconomic projections 3. It emphasises that the end of the RRF is expected to weigh
on public investment in 2027, while it anticipates an acceleration in business investment. Furthermore, RRF funds are being absorbed on
the ground only gradually. Since most disbursements are concentrated toward the end of the programme, the effects of the RRF will extend

beyond 2026.
guillaume.a.derrien@bnpparibas.com

Editorial 
Opportunities from the bond sell-off  
Strategy summary: The global bond sell-off is creating tactical 
and medium-term opportunities. In the US, September’s yield 
surge has improved the risk -reward in 10 -year government 
bonds, presenting an attractive entry point for investors with a 
6-12-month horizon. We believe bond and money markets are 
overly hawkish about the Fed outlook, given disinflation is likely 
to return as impact of oil prices and tariffs ease next year. 
In equities, we stick to quality, especially sectors riding the AI -
led earnings boom. The upcoming Q3 earnings season is likely 
to show AI -investments are powering sectors beyond the 
technology and communications services, such as financials, 
materials and power and electrification. We remain constructive 
on the semiconductor and memory segments. We also expect 
the US Dollar to peak in the next few weeks along with short -
to-medium-term US bond yields. This creates opportunities for 
AUD corporate bonds, Emerging Market USD bonds and gold. 
Attractive risk -reward in US 10 -year government bonds.  
The US 10-year bond yield rose more than 50bps in September 
(biggest monthly rise since 2022) and 115bps this year, amid 
expectations of economic resilience, higher Fed rates, fuel -
driven inflation, AI-related capital demand and fiscal concerns. 
Valuations appear close to fair value, improving the 6-12-month 
risk-reward. Bond arithmetic suggests losses of less than 1% if 
the yield rises to 6%, vs. more than 10% gains if it falls to 4.5%. 
Resilient US economic activity, but softer -than-expected 
inflation. September data showed robust US employment 
(jobless claims, ADP) and core capital-goods orders. However, 
consumer confidence remains depressed, real disposable 
income is flat and households are drawing on savings to sustain 
spending. Against this divided backdrop, core PCE inflation - 
the Fed’s preferred gauge - was softer than expected at 0.2% 
m/m and 3.0% y/y. A revised method to measure PCE inflation 
also indicate that annual core PCE inflation was 35bps lower 
than previously reported, narrowing the gap with CPI inflation. 
Markets appear overly hawkish on the Fed. Money markets 
price about 85bps of tightening over the next 12 months. A 
strong labour market (September’s non -farm payrolls data is 
due tonight) may justify one or two further hikes, but aggressive 
tightening after the sharp rise in bond yields – which has 
already tightened financial conditions - could threaten financial 
stability. US small- and mid-cap equities have already fallen 8–
9% from recent peaks over the past couple of months, 
materially underperforming large caps, as energy and 
borrowing-cost concerns intensify. W hile some Fed speakers 
remain hawkish, we  agree with New York Fed President 
Williams’ that there is no urgency to tighten. We see little 
chance of an October Fed rate hike and expect inflation data 
for Sep-Nov determining whether the Fed moves in December. 
Stay with quality equities and AI beneficiaries.  US large 
caps have absorbed the rise in bond yields relatively well, 
supported by expectations of robust earnings growth led by AI-
related investment. We continue to favour quality companies 
and sectors benefiting from this AI cycle. Semiconductors and 
memory remain preferred exposures: structural AI demand 
should extend the memory upcycle, while long -term supply 
agreements provide better revenue visibility. The opportunity is 
also broadening beyond technology and communication 
services into financials, materials, power and electrification. 
Opportunities in AUD corporate bonds and gold.  The USD 
rebound alongside higher US yields offers a tactical opportunity 
to add higher -yielding Emerging Market USD and non -USD 
bonds. We remain positive on AUD corporate bonds, which 
offer a yield premium over comparable US credit. Our 
constructive medium-term AUD/USD view could add returns for 
USD-based investors over the next 6 –12 months. We would 
also use gold’s recent pullback to add exposure, supported by 
sustained Emerging Market central bank demand. We continue 
to view USD 4,000/oz as important technical support for gold.  
—  Rajat Bhattacharya

### Page 4

Standard Chartered Bank 
WS Global CIO Office | 2 October 2026

Top client questions 
  Do you expect more RBA rate hikes after its less hawkish 
guidance? What is your view on the AUD and AUD bonds? 
Our view: We expect the RBA to leave rates unchanged, but 
retain the option to hike again should inflation intensify. We 
maintain our medium-term constructive AUD view and maintain 
our opportunistically bullish AUD corporate bonds idea. 
Rationale: The RBA’s forward guidance remains data dependent, 
explicitly allowing further tightening if needed as inflation remains too 
high.  At 4.60%, Australia’s cash rate is currently 60–85bp above the 
Fed’s 3.75–4.00% range. Thus, even with our two additional 25bp s 
Fed hikes expectation, Australian rates would hold a modest positive 
rate differential. We expect the AUD to strengthen once the Fed 
signals that US rates are near their peak, which should allow resilient 
Australian fundamentals to reassert themselves. Key risks are 
persistently high US yields, weaker China growth or a sharper 
deterioration in global risk sentiment. 
We believe risk-reward has become more attractive for AUD bonds 
following the bear -flattening of the AUD yield curve. Markets are 
already pricing in one more RBA hike through end-2027, presenting 
an attractive opportunity to earn a yield even as increasingly 
restrictive financial conditions reinforc e disinflation over time. 
Meanwhile, the positive interest rate differential with the US supports 
the currency outlook, supporting the case for AUD bonds. 
—  Vincent Tan, Senior Investment Strategist 
— Cedric Lam, Senior Investment Strategist 
— Iris Yuen, Investment Strategist 
  What does the incoming US data tell us about the path for 
US 10-year bond yields from here? 
Our view: The risk -reward in US 10 -year yields has turned 
attractive following their move higher amid incoming US data. 
Rationale: Recent US data has indeed started to show softer-than-
expected inflation. However, market technicals are likely temporarily 
delaying a move lower in bond yields, with reports suggesting the  
rise in yields is creating forced selling amongst hedge funds and real 
estate investment trusts. Nevertheless, we do not expect this to be 
an extended selloff. We have initiated an opportunistic bullish idea 
on US 10-year government bonds this week and continue to believe 
this idea offers an attractive risk/reward on a 6-12-month horizon as 
yields become attractive enough to entice buyer flow to return. On a 
broader allocation basis, we retain our structural preference for 3-7-
year maturities in bonds. 
— Cedric Lam, Senior Investment Strategist

Australia-US interest rate differentials and positive

### Page 5

Standard Chartered Bank 
WS Global CIO Office | 2 October 2026

Top client questions (cont’d) 
  How should investors position in the GBP ahead of the UK 
budget announcement next month? 
Our view: We maintain a medium-term constructive GBP view, 
but keep near-term exposure measured ahead of the 28 October 
Budget. We would view any pre -Budget weakness as an 
opportunity to gradually build GBP exposure. 
Rationale: The government’s decision to adjust the pension triple 
lock from 2030 is an early indication of greater willingness to tackle 
longer-term spending pressures, reinforcing Chancellor Healey’s 
commitment to fiscal discipline and meeting the fiscal rules. Markets 
are likely to scrutinise the Budget closely for evidence that this 
discipline extends to near -term tax and spending decisions. A 
credible Budget should help contain pressure on UK government 
bond yields and support GBP. Any fiscal slippage remains a key risk.  
Near term, we prefer measured GBP exposure as elevated US yields 
and repricing towards further Fed tightening could continue to 
support the USD. Over the medium term, we expect GBP to rebound 
as the Fed eventually signals the peak of its tightening cycle. 
—  Vincent Tan, Senior Investment Strategist 
—  Iris Yuen, Investment Strategist 
  How would different US mid-term election outcome 
scenarios affect the outlook for US equities? 
Our view: We believe election outcomes will have a limited 
impact over the next 12 months. We remain positive on US 
equities driven by fundamental earnings growth.  
Rationale: Over the past 50 years, the ruling US President’s party 
has retained control of both chambers of Congress on only two 
occasions (1978 and 2002). All other elections result ed in an 
opposition sweep or a divided government.  
Based on the latest prediction markets, it appears that an opposition 
sweep (both the House and Senate flips from Republican to 
Democrats) is more likely than a divided government ( in which the 
House flips to Democrats , while the Senate remains under 
Republican control). A scenario where Republicans retain a majority 
is least likely per prediction markets. 
While mid -term elections may introduce near -term volatility, we 
caution against overreaction. Historically, the average return for the 
S&P 500 following an opposition sweep is 17% over the next 12 
months, but the average return following a divided government is not 
significantly worse, at 15%. We prefer to base our investment views 
on fundamental earnings growth, where investor attention should 
focus on the upcoming Q3 earnings season. 
—  Jason Wong, Senior Equity Analyst

### Page 6

Standard Chartered Bank 
WS Global CIO Office | 2 October 2026

Top client questions (cont’d) 
  What is the outlook for  the memory chip and broader 
semiconductors industry? 
Our view: We remain positive on semiconductors and memory 
chips. Structural AI demand should prolong the memory 
upcycle while long-term agreements improve revenue visibility. 
Rationale: Recent industry commentary points to tighter memory 
supply-demand conditions through 2028, reinforcing our view that AI 
demand is likely to extend the memory upcycle. Furthermore, adoption 
of long-term agreements improves revenue and earnings visibility. 
Despite debates around “Pacing the Frontier”, we have seen a flurry 
of new model releases, along with the introduction of new consumer 
AI agents capable of comparing prices online and making 
purchases. These are early days, but the path is open for a business-
to-agent (B2A) economy where businesses increasingly interact with 
AI assistants acting on customers’ behalf. As these applications are 
increasingly inference intensive, wider adoption should support 
demand for memory, with its share of global semiconductor 
revenues projected to rise from 30.7% in 2025 to 66.7% in 2027. 
Against this backdrop, we maintain our Opportunistic idea on Global 
Semiconductors. Given memory  chips’ cyclical nature, investors 
should avoid overconcentration in memory names and maintain a 
diversified exposure across Big Tech and semiconductors. 
—  Ryan Goh, Investment Strategist 
  Does a US bond yield stress test raise the risk to your 
constructive view on US equities? 
Our view: We have a target for the S&P 500 index of 8400 over 
the next 12 months. A scenario of a sustained rise in the US 10-
year government bond yield to 5.6% could lead to negative 
returns from the current S&P500 level (7,666). 
Rationale: Our base case 12-month target for the S&P 500 is 8,400, 
which assumes 2027 S&P 500 EPS of USD 420 and a 20x forward 
P/E multiple. A rise in bond yields would have an impact on equity 
valuations, as a higher discount rate reduces the present value of 
future earnings. W e stress -test our target under different 10 -year 
yield and earnings scenarios.  
A rise in bond yields would arguably be associated with a stronger 
growth environment and hence, even higher earnings. However, 
assuming unchanged EPS at our base case of USD 420, a 
(sustained) rise in the 10 -year bond yield to 5.6% would lead to 
negative returns from the current level  if not offset by a further 
improvement in earnings growth . However, in o ur base case  we 
expect the 10-year yield is more likely to fall from here. 
—  Fook Hien Yap, Senior Investment Strategist

## Document 272: Persistent sources of inflation and the RBNZ (2026-10-05)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Persistent sources of inflation and the RBNZ (2026-10-05).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-10-05
Pages: 8
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

1
Westpac New Zealand Economics
A lack of headroom: The RBNZ, oil prices and administered costs.
• New Zealand households have faced a run of
large cost increases in recent years related
to administered charges and parts of the
economy that are less exposed to competition,
such as electricity charges and local
council rates.
• Those cost increases have continued for an
extended period, making them hard for the
RBNZ to look through.
• This has also meant that the RBNZ had little

headroom to absorb upside inflation surprises
even before the recent spike in oil prices.
• We expect that there will be ongoing sizeable
increases in many of these costs. That is a
key reason why we expect domestic (non-
tradables) inflation will be higher than the
RBNZ expects over the longer-term.
• That ongoing firmness in domestic inflation
is also a reason why we expect the RBNZ
will ultimately need to raise the OCR by
more than they assumed in their September

policy Statement.
• But getting inflation down to target levels
in the face of an oil price spike and high
administered prices won’t be easy and involves
some tough trade-offs. Administered prices
tend to be less responsive to changes in
economic conditions. As a result, interest
rate hikes are likely to have a bigger impact
on parts of the economy that are not the main
drivers of inflation.
Inflation is high for reasons beyond
oil prices.
Inflation in New Zealand rose to 4.1% in the year to June,

the highest it’s been in three years. And it’s set to remain
above 3% until at least mid-2027.
Much of the recent strength in inflation has been due
to the spike in oil prices since the outbreak of war in the
Middle East. However, even before the spike in fuel costs,
inflation had been running hot for an extended period:
inflation has been above 3% since mid-2025, and it’s now
been above the RBNZ’s 2% target midpoint for five years.
That persistent firmness in inflation is particularly notable

as, based on RBNZ estimates, the New Zealand economy
has had a negative output gap since September 2024,
with softness in economic growth and a weak labour
market. Those conditions would typically be associated
with more moderate inflation than we’ve seen.

Looking beneath the surface, much of the strength
in inflation over the past year has been related to
administered costs, such as government charges, and
price increases in parts of the economy that are less
1 Oct 2026 | Satish Ranchhod, Senior Economist | +64 9 336 5668 | +64 21 710 852 | satish.ranchhod@westpac.co.nz
ECONOMIC BULLETIN
Persistent inflation and the RBNZ.

### Page 2

2
Westpac New Zealand Economics
exposed to competitive pressures. For instance, over the
past year, local council rates rose nearly 9%, electricity
prices rose by 12% and health insurance premiums rose
by 19%.
The less competitive nature of some of these sectors
means they aren’t very responsive to changes in interest
rates. Instead, they are most likely to behave in a “cost-
plus” manner. And in some cases, recent years have seen
large increases in operating costs that are now being

passed on to consumers.
These aren’t one-off cost rises the RBNZ can
look through.
Just as the RBNZ might look through a one-off spike
in oil prices, there’s a reasonable argument for looking
through a one-off rise in administered prices that pushes
up inflation. For instance, a one-off increase in vehicle
registration costs might boost inflation temporarily, but
isn’t something the RBNZ would respond to.
However, the issue here is that the increases we’re seeing

are not one-off price rises. We’ve seen large increases
spread across a range of administered costs for several
years now (see chart on page 4). That’s been reflected
in measures of core inflation, which strip out swings in
volatile items like fuel and instead track the underlying
trend in prices. For example, excluding the rise in food
and fuel prices, inflation in the year to June was 2.9%,
and it’s been running close to or above 3% for five years

now. That’s meant that the RBNZ had little headroom to
absorb upside inflation surprises even before the recent
spike in fuel costs.

We expect that there will be continued large increases
in some administered costs. For instance, in the case
of local council rates, many councils will need to fund
significant infrastructure spending over the coming

years. There have been efforts to limit the extent of those
increases, such as the Government’s cap on council
rates. However, that doesn’t change the underlying
reason for the large rates increases in recent years, such
as increased spending on essential infrastructure. And if
councils can’t raise the required revenue through rates,
they may have to look at other approaches like user
pays. That could shift costs for households, rather than
reducing them.

Why does this matter?
It’s important to remember that the RBNZ’s focus is not

individual prices in the CPI (like fuel prices or council
rates), but the overall level of inflation. In essence, it
doesn’t matter why inflation is elevated – all that matters
is that it is elevated and how persistent inflation will be.
Right now, with core inflation already elevated, it’s hard
for the RBNZ to look through the large and continuing
cost increases that we’ve seen across a range of areas
that are less exposed to competition. And given the risk

of ongoing large increases in these costs, we expect that
domestic (non-tradables) inflation will be higher than the
RBNZ has assumed over the longer-term.
That’s part of the reason why we continue to expect that
the RBNZ will need to raise the Official Cash Rate by
more than they had assumed in their September policy
Statement. We’re forecasting a 25bp rate rise at the
RBNZ’s December meeting and expect the OCR will peak
at 4% next year. That risk is being compounded by the

protracted nature of the current rise in oil prices, along
with other risks such as El Niño which could boost food
prices over the coming year.
However, costs like oil prices, electricity charges, and
administered charges like council rates are not responsive
to changes in interest rates. As a result, when inflation
pressures are centred on such areas, the RBNZ needs
to work harder to keep overall inflation in check. It does
this by leaning against inflation in areas of the economy

that are more responsive to interest rates to bring
aggregate inflation pressures down towards target in a
timely manner. But rather than leaning against the source
of inflation pressures, the impact of tighter policy will
be to dampen demand and price-setting in other more

### Page 3

3
Westpac New Zealand Economics
interest rate-sensitive parts of the economy such as the
construction sector or discretionary retail spending.
This is an uncomfortable but necessary trade-off. Tighter
policy will weigh on discretionary spending, the labour
market and near-term growth, and would affect prices in
parts of the economy that are not the drivers of inflation.
A higher OCR also means increases in households’ debt-
servicing costs, and for some households, that will be

very challenging.
However, looking through or not responding to these
sorts of ongoing large cost increases would still leave
households dealing with significant increases in overall
living costs. In the long run, that could be more damaging
for households and the economy.
